DOJ steps up its investigation into the Dobbs leak

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

This week, during the first half of the November argument session, the justices heard oral arguments in four cases and quickly declined a request from the Trump administration to intervene in a voting dispute in California. However, headlines about these developments were overshadowed by Tuesday’s midterm elections. The blue wave brought new significance to debates over court reform and when Justices Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas might retire.

Today, the justices will meet in a private conference to discuss cases and vote on petitions for review. Orders from today’s conference are expected on Monday at 9:30 a.m. EST.

On Thursday, the Supreme Court denied two requests for a stay of execution from Jesse Guardado, who was sentenced to death for the 2004 murder of Jackie Malone. Hours later, Guardado was executed in Florida.

On Wednesday, the justices heard argument in Guerrero v. Johnson, on the limited circumstances in which a state prisoner can return to federal court for a second round of post-conviction review. Here is the link to our Case Preview.

On Tuesday, the justices heard argument in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy, a religious discrimination challenge brought by Catholic preschools in Colorado over the state’s requirement that all schools participating in its universal preschool program must admit everyone – including LGBTQ children and children with LGBTQ parents. Here are the links to our Case Preview and Argument Analysis.

On Monday, the justices heard argument in Maxwell v. Dinis, on a federal inmate’s ability to use a habeas statute, 28 U.S.C. § 2241, to contest the Federal Bureau of Prisons’ refusal to accelerate his transfer to a halfway house or home confinement. Here are the links to our Case Preview and Argument Analysis.

The justices also heard argument on Monday in Younge v. Fulton Judicial Circuit District Attorney’s Office, Georgia, on whether an affirmative defense may be asserted as a basis for summary judgment if not presented earlier in a pleading. Here are the links to our Case Preview and Argument Analysis.

Monday morning, the Trump administration returned to the Supreme Court’s interim docket, asking the justices to intervene in its efforts to keep California from counting ballots cast by individuals who it contends are noncitizens. Just hours later, the court denied the request. There were no noted dissents.

Later on Monday, the court released its calendar for the January argument session, which will begin on Monday, Jan. 11.

On Oct. 21, the Trump administration asked the court to allow it to resume construction of its border wall in the Big Bend region of southwestern Texas. The request has been fully briefed since last Friday, and the court’s decision could come at any time.

Looking ahead, the justices will hear arguments on Monday in two cases: Nielsen v. Watanabe, on whether and when federal officials can be held liable for constitutional violations; and Hoffmann v. WBI Energy Transmission, Inc., on whether the “just compensation” paid to land owners losing property to a pipeline project should be determined by referring to state law, which often provides for a higher payment than what the Constitution requires.

Jodi Kantor and Michael S. Schmidt, The New York Times

In May 2022, Politico released a leaked draft of the Supreme Court’s decision in Dobbs v. Jackson Women’s Health Organization, which overturned the right to abortion. The leak was “a seismic breach of confidentiality” and led to “an internal inquiry by the court,” although “no leaker has ever been publicly identified.” But The New York Times reported on Thursday that the Justice Department is now “pursuing a criminal investigation into who leaked” the draft opinion and that this “investigation has ramped up in recent weeks as prosecutors have sent grand jury subpoenas for testimony and sought information from people who worked at the court at the time of the leaks.”

Tuesday’s midterm elections “all but guaranteed” President Donald Trump won’t be able to put a fourth justice on the Supreme Court. Beginning in January, Democrats will have “a Senate majority—with a several-seat cushion—that will make it easy to block Trump judicial nominees, especially for any Supreme Court vacancies that might arise in 2027 or 2028,” according to The Wall Street Journal. Faced with this reality, “[s]ome legal observers have floated a last-ditch scenario in which [Clarence] Thomas or [Samuel] Alito could announce their retirement in the coming days and give Trump and the outgoing Republican Senate a chance to quickly install a successor before the newly elected Congress is seated on Jan. 3.” But the Journal described this scenario as “exceedingly unlikely,” in large part because neither Alito nor Thomas appear interested in retiring. And it’s not “clear that a Supreme Court nominee could get through the lame-duck Congress even if there were a retirement, especially because some moderate Republican senators are on their way out of office and blame Trump for losing their seats.”

In a Thursday interview with the Associated Press, Senate Democratic leader Chuck Schumer said “that Democrats will not allow President Donald Trump to put ‘a right-winger’ on the Supreme Court once they take power.” He “would not say if he would hold a Supreme Court vacancy open until after the next presidential election, as former Senate Republican leader Mitch McConnell did in 2016. But he said that Democrats are ‘not going to allow any crappy nominees’ to be confirmed to the Supreme Court.” “The bottom line is we’re not going to allow a right-winger on the court,” Schumer said.

In a column for Bloomberg, Noah Feldman contended that Tuesday’s election results “should cause Democrats to reconsider the wisdom of plans to pack the court or strip it of jurisdiction over certain issues.” It should no longer feel as urgent to make such changes, according to Feldman, because “[i]f a Democrat wins the presidency in 2028 and the Democrats hold the Senate, the Democrats will have a chance to flip the court from 6-3 conservative to 5-4 liberal” simply by filling predicted or expected vacancies. The reform proposals floated in recent months “make little sense if Democrats take seriously the prospect of a liberal recapture of the institution,” Feldman wrote.

The Supreme Court heard argument on Monday in Maxwell v. Dinis on whether a federal inmate may use a habeas statute to contest the Federal Bureau of Prisons’ refusal to accelerate his transfer to a halfway house or home confinement.

The argument on Nov. 10 in Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards brings the justices a complex constitutional problem: Does the Constitution allow Congress to delegate to the executive branch – as opposed to the courts – the authority to adjudicate the obligation of an employer to comply with employment agreements it makes with temporary immigrant workers?

In his Empirical SCOTUS column, Adam Feldman offered an in-depth analysis of Justice Elena Kagan, examining not only how she votes, but also how she chooses to agree and disagree.

In her Ratio Decidendi column, Stephanie Barclay revisited Tuesday’s argument in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy, noting that the discussion put a spotlight on 1990’s Employment Division v. Smith, a controversial ruling that made it easier for the government to overcome free exercise challenges. The ruling in St. Mary, according to Barclay, “will determine how much is left of” Smith, which has already fallen out of favor with the court.

In his Relist Watch column on Wednesday, John Elwood explored four petitions for review that will get a second look from the justices at today’s conference. Three concern a long-running dispute over Alabama’s congressional districts and the fourth asks whether using a car makes an otherwise local kidnapping a federal crime – even when the car never crosses a state line.

In his Courtly Observations column on Tuesday, Erwin Chemerinsky explored Nielsen v. Watanabe, in which the court is considering under what circumstances federal officers may be sued for money damages for constitutional violations, noting that the case comes at a time when efforts “to hold ICE agents accountable” have taken on new urgency.

On Oct. 19, the Supreme Court granted review in Association for Education Fairness v. Montgomery County Board of Education, a case on race and school admissions. In his AV Ristorante column on Monday, Brian Fitzpatrick explained why he believes “it could be the final nail in affirmative action’s coffin.”

Sarah Isgur and David French discuss the Texas Supreme Court’s blue wave, the possibility of a majority-Democrat Supreme Court, and one of the biggest Supreme Court cases of the term: St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy.

Friday’s A Closer Look is typically a judicial biography. This week we cover Justice Peter V. Daniel, who served on the court from 1841/2 to 1860.

Like some of the other justices we’ve covered, Justice Peter V. Daniel is one that “[f]ew recall or have ever heard of.” Those who know of Daniel have not been complimentary. He was called “a brooding proslavery fanatic” by law professor Earl Maltz, and a 1965 book review likened him to the notoriously bigoted Justice James C. McReynolds, minus McReynolds’ occasional “charm.”

Daniel was born in April 1784 on his family’s estate in Stafford County, Virginia. After studying with private tutors and a brief stint at the College of New Jersey (now Princeton University), he read law in Richmond under Edmund Randolph, President George Washington’s attorney general and secretary of state. In 1810, Daniel married Randolph’s daughter, Lucy.

In November 1808, Daniel fought a duel in Maryland against businessman John Seddon; Seddon subsequently died from his injuries. This did not appear to significantly slow Daniel’s career, however: He subsequently spent two one-year terms in the Virginia House of Delegates, a tenure his biographer, John P. Frank, judged as “utterly undistinguished.” In 1812, Daniel joined Virginia’s Council of State, which advised the governor, and from 1818 to 1835 served as lieutenant governor. His “friends” “apparently lacked any real warmth or affection for him, complaining of his ‘frozen deportment,’” which played out literally: In the winter of 1813, Daniel reportedly balked at spending a modest sum to keep militiamen warm, despite their officer’s warning that the men’s lives were at stake, on the reasoning that Virginia state law made the soldiers responsible for their own clothing.

Daniel’s Richmond law practice was modestly successful, but he had other aspirations. Through the Richmond Junto (a Virginia Democratic-Republican network) he organized and led the state’s Jacksonians, and, along with then-New York governor Martin Van Buren, built an “Albany-Richmond axis” which backed Andrew Jackson for president. Daniel subsequently turned down Jackson’s offer of attorney general. In 1836, however, he accepted a federal judgeship in eastern Virginia, Jackson’s reward for his party loyalty and his support for the president’s war on the national bank.

When Justice Philip Barbour died in February 1841, Van Buren had roughly a week left in office and rushed to nominate Daniel to the court. Aware of Daniel’s reputation, Whig senators tried to stall the vote, at one point walking out together to leave the chamber without a quorum, while Democratic leaders searched the capital for their own absent senators. Daniel was ultimately confirmed 22-5 in a midnight vote. The exact date he took the oath is unclear; the Federal Judicial Center lists it in January 1842 “based on questionable authority,” while one biography entry has him joining the court in December 1841.

After he joined the bench, Daniel’s circuit assignment included Arkansas and Mississippi, which involved arduous trips from Washington. While circuit riding in 1851, for example, Daniel rode in a canal boat for two days to get from D.C. to Pittsburgh (and was not a fan of the other “vulgar and filthy” passengers).

On the court itself, close to one in three of Daniel’s written opinions was a dissent, and during his tenure his solo dissents outnumbered any other justice’s by more than two to one. He fought against extending federal admiralty power to inland waters, and when the court did just that in 1852’s Genesee Chief v. Fitzhugh, Daniel was the lone dissenter. Perhaps his best-known majority opinion came in 1848’s West River Bridge Co. v. Dix, which put Daniel opposite the famed lawyer and statesman Daniel Webster. Daniel detested Webster so much that, after meeting him, he reported his hand “was actually contaminated by contact with his.” In the case, Vermont had taken over a privately chartered toll bridge, paid compensation, and opened it as a free road, but the bridge company argued that the takeover breached the contract clause of the Constitution. Writing for the majority, Daniel held that the franchise was ordinary property that the state could take through eminent domain.

Daniel, who was a slaveholder, concurred in 1842’s Prigg v. Pennsylvania, which held that the federal Fugitive Slave Act preempted Pennsylvania’s ban on recapturing formerly enslaved persons. After Van Buren joined the antislavery Free Soilers, Daniel’s friendship with him ended, and Daniel reportedly swore he would never again set foot north of Maryland (he came to blame even bad weather on the North). In Dred Scott v. Sandford, Daniel argued that freed slaves could not be citizens because they had once been held as property and that the Northwest Ordinance of 1787 (which, among many other things, had kept slavery out of land north and west of the Ohio River) was unconstitutional, thus taking a more extreme position than even Chief Justice Roger Taney, who wrote the majority opinion.

Daniel’s first wife, Lucy, died in 1847, and his second wife died in 1857 after a candle accidentally lit her clothes on fire. Daniel died in Richmond in May 1860 following a lengthy illness. In February 1861, Republican senators blocked President James Buchanan’s pick to replace him, Jeremiah Black, making the seat one of the three vacancies that President Abraham Lincoln filled in 1862. It stayed open until July 1862, when Lincoln chose Justice Samuel Miller.

In a December 1860 memorial, the attorney general said that Daniel “held the pen of a ready writer,” but “was sometimes in danger of going too directly to his purpose and treating with too much contempt the obstacles that intervened.”

JUSTICE THOMAS: “Does Atkins ever stop being a new rule?”

MR. SCHEPERS: “In our view, Atkins was new at the time it was decided.”

JUSTICE THOMAS: “We're talking about now, though.”

MR. SCHEPERS: “That – we believe that Atkins was new based off of the character of the decision in 2002 and that it remains new now.”

JUSTICE THOMAS: “What about Marbury v. Madison?”

Aryna Sabalenka, Coco Gauff, Mirra Andreeva, and More on Winning, Losing, Living, and Dreaming

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ahead of the season-ending WTA Finals tournament, kicking off Sunday at Indian Wells, the world’s eight highest-ranked women’s tennis players gathered for a fashion shoot and an easy hang. There, they talked exclusively to Vogue about their biggest wins, their toughest losses, their secret lives away from the court (pole dancing, UFC, baking cakes—who knew?)—and what they like to wear.

HEATED RIVALRY? Elina Svitolina, in Tom Ford, scores a snack while Rybakina, Gauff, and Muchova come away empty-handed.

The Indian Wells final was one of the best, because it was a really tough match, and I was a couple of match points down—I had to overcome a lot of things, and I got the win.

Obviously the Australian and US Open finals—in Australia [against Elena Rybakina], I had a break in the third set and felt like everything was going my way, but then it slipped, so I was really, really disappointed with that one. I should have won that one, but I didn’t. The US Open, not so much—[Rybakina] played really incredibly, and I didn’t.

When you walk out onto the court for a match, you’ve got headphones on. What are you listening to?

Sometimes nothing—sometimes I just like to block out the noise and stay in my thoughts. Sometimes I just put on some melodies that make me feel fired up. It’s always different—it just depends on my mood. I have a lot of favorite artists. I like Beyoncé. I like Rihanna. I like Dua Lipa, Tate McRae, Teddy Swims, Eminem, Justin Timberlake, 50 Cent, Britney Spears. I just need to rotate.

If you didn’t play any sport, what would you do for a career?

I think boxing or modeling—they’re so different, but both are things that I love.

Is there a sport other than tennis that you watch or play?

In Dubai—it’s actually crazy to say—but for the first time in my life I went to watch a UFC fight, and I became obsessed. I’m watching lots of fights now, and my entire algorithm is UFC and fighters. I became a huge fan of Khabib [Nurmagomedov, the undefeated UFC legend]. So, yeah, I’m crazy about UFC. I also like ice hockey, and I like to watch basketball and Formula One. I’d like to watch more MotoGP, but I cannot watch it because I’m like, How do you guys do that? It’s so dangerous. I feel so tight watching them race—I’m so scared for them.

I know that you like to get dressed up and that you’re no stranger to going big. You like a big heel, yeah?

Well, I love the way my body looks. But walking, as an athlete? It’s like a workout for me. I had to walk Vogue World in Milan, and I was just dying, because I’m not used to walking in high heels. But still, that was the best experience of my life.

Probably the US Open. I won the Australian Open at the beginning of the year, but the US Open just felt a little bit bigger this year, and New York is such a special city.

What’s your best weapon on the tennis court?

Just the aggressive style of my game. My serve can put me in a position where I can attack from the first ball and be dominant from the beginning of the point, and when that works, it doesn’t really matter who is on the other side.

When you’re away from the court, do you have a guilty pleasure, an indulgence, or a hobby?

I love sweets. With all the travel, I’m very sensitive, so it’s really not easy for me to just let it go and eat whatever I want, but yeah. When I get a chance, I kind of destroy myself—not just sweets, but everything baked: croissants, pain au chocolat.

HOLDING COURT From left: Karolina Muchova in Balenciaga. Sabalenka in a Nike jacket and Gucci skirt and shoes, Comme Si socks. Linda Noskova wears Missoni shorts and a jacket and white leather trench by The Frankie Shop; Miista boots. Pegula wears Chanel.

I played a really great match against Amanda [Anisimova] in Australia to make the semis, and then winning the Dubai title—being able to beat [Elina] Svitolina in the final—was really great. Everyone knows Dubai, and it’s cool to say, “Oh yeah, I won that title in Dubai.” I also played a really great match against Iva Jovic to make the quarterfinals in Wimbledon. I felt like I was able to go up two levels and kind of take the match from her, and to be able to do that against a younger player in a big situation felt great.

Did you have a hero or role model when you were growing up?

I didn’t watch a ton of tennis growing up—I was playing tennis—but my mom read me a lot of books about players, biographies and autobiographies, and I remember being drawn to Martina Hingis and Mary Pierce when my mom would read me their stories.

What’s your best weapon on the tennis court?

I’m very good at staying even-keeled and not wasting a lot of energy. It drains me when I’m wasting a lot of emotional energy. Some people play off of that and can handle it great, but I feel that gives me too many ups and downs.

One piece of advice for an average club player?

Sometimes I think people see pro players hit the ball and they’re like, Oh—I need to hit it harder. And you absolutely do not. I think that learning how to hit your medium rally ball that you can make 85% of the time is really important, because that’s something you can build your game around.

How do you get pumped up—or calmed down and focused—before a match?

I used to meditate quite a bit, and I feel like I can always go back to it if I’m feeling a nervous or anxious type of energy. Then I move on to just focusing on breathwork, which I think is so important for us when we’re on the court, too. Sometimes if you focus on your breath and let everything else just kind of happen, everything starts flowing and you get more instinctual, which I think is how you always want to play.

Do you have a guilty pleasure or something that you indulge yourself in when you’re away from tennis?

I play a lot of Call of Duty. Hours. I have a gaming laptop and I have a PS5 at home. One of my best friends and her boyfriend play, and my husband and I play with them all the time. I play with my brother and my sister a lot, too. I was a big Sims kid—sometimes I’ll whip that out and play for three days straight and then put it away for a year—and I went through a big Grand Theft Auto phase. I’ve heard mixed things about the new GTA—people are questioning the graphics—but let’s just say I’m interested.

If you didn’t play tennis—or any sport—what kind of job would you have?

When I was younger, I got really into hockey and always said that I wanted to be the first female GM of a hockey team. But now that I know too much, being a GM sounds really stressful.

When you’re getting dressed for a big night out, do you have a favorite piece, or something that you love that’s important to you?

I like traveling with a good dress and then elevating that with nice shoes or a purse or a jacket. And I’m definitely a jewelry person—that’s always been my thing.

You’re wearing Chanel here—were you a fan before this?

I got a really nice Chanel bag that I wore throughout my whole wedding weekend a few years ago, and I have a really nice pair of Chanel shoes, and another Chanel bag in a [Buffalo] Bills kind of blue that I’ve worn to games.

The Rome title—I had won there before twice, but winning there again as a mom, eight years later, was really a proud moment.

Did you have a hero or a role model growing up?

I really loved watching Andre Agassi—I loved his style of play and his presence on the court, and as a young player I always tried to imitate him, in a way: his spirit, his energy, the way he created the game on his own terms.

How do you get pumped up—or calmed down and focused—before a match?

Over the years, I’ve tried different tactics: Sometimes meditation works for me, sometimes it’s watching motivational videos, sometimes music—AC/DC, Eminem, Red Hot Chili Peppers. I also really love the spirit of Kobe Bryant and the way that he kind of went into this state.

Yeah, exactly—he was just really focused on the goal, the mission.

If you didn’t play any sport, what kind of job do you think you would have?

I would be a chef. Cooking really brings me joy, and when I’m home, I like to cook for the family. My grandmother and my mom are great cooks—I think it’s in our culture to prepare something nice.

CHEERING SECTION Clockwise, from top left: Pegula, Svitolina, Noskova, and Muchova are all gunning for number-one.

I mean, you don’t have to, but if I were you I probably would.

Yeah. From any perspective, that final was mentally and physically one of the toughest matches that I’ve ever played in my life.

How do you get pumped up—or calmed down and focused—before a match?

I usually like to calm down—I don’t really like to do anything right before my match, like listen to music or whatever, but a few hours before that I like to read: horror, something historical, a biography. I like a lot of different genres.

Do you have a guilty pleasure or something that you indulge yourself in when you’re away from tennis?

In my off-season, I like to travel to different places to see different cultures, different people, different languages. Last year I went to Zanzibar, in Tanzania, and volunteered at a local kindergarten for a week or 10 days, which was short but sweet, and I would love to go back there again.

Is there a sport other than tennis that you either watch or play?

I’ve been a huge fan of WWE since I was 14 or 15. It’s pretty weird for a European kid—I watched it on YouTube, but then when I was in America for the first time I put on some random channel and saw the wrestling and I was super pumped about it. That was my first time watching it live. I love Charlotte Flair, Sasha Banks, and some men as well, like the Undertaker and John Cena. Charlotte Flair was in my player’s box at the US Open this year—she’s obviously super strong and such an athlete, but at the same time, she’s a gorgeous woman, and so cool.

The finals in Doha—and playing doubles with Serena [Williams, in Berlin] was also so important, even if we lost in the first round. But it was a special match—just to have the chance to stand with such a champion as Serena and to practice with her.

What’s your best weapon on the tennis court?

My creativity—the way I can make the court very uncomfortable for my opponents…and for myself, sometimes, too.

How do you get pumped up—or calmed down and focused—before a match?

I listen to songs—different ones at every tournament. It can even be a slow song that brings you some feelings, but I always play that one song, and then I’m over it and go to another one. Lately it’s been just beats, fast songs, but it can be something from Beyoncé or Harry Styles, or some Czech pop songs.

Do you have a guilty pleasure or something that you indulge yourself in when you’re away from tennis?

I eat crazy food. I have a sweet tooth, so candy, chocolate, ice cream, pancakes; but I also like junk food, hamburgers, and everything that I can’t have when I play. I also play guitar—just to relax, as a hobby. I’ve been playing for about nine years, both acoustic and electric. I have a beautiful Takamine acoustic and a really nice Fender Stratocaster that my parents gave me for my birthday this year.

Is there a sport other than tennis that you watch or play?

My dad and my brother are both footballers—my dad is now a coach—so I watch Czech football, and when I’m in America I like to go watch the NBA or NHL.

When you’re getting dressed for a big night out, do you have a favorite piece, or something that you love that’s important to you?

I like to have a nice bag and jewelry, but overall I’m going for effortless and casual. I like simple pieces that look like I didn’t try too hard to put them together, even if I actually did.

SHINE ON Sabalenka, Gauff, Rybakina, and Mirra Andreeva (in Chanel) take to the stands instead of the court.

Probably the US Open quarterfinal against Mirra [Andreeva]—I saved two match points. I wasn’t really playing that well, but I fought really hard for that one. I also think the energy in New York just gives you an extra motivation to go the extra mile and never give up. And I’m always not giving up, but sometimes home court advantage is a thing.

Did you have a hero or a role model growing up?

The Williams sisters—Serena and Venus—are definitely my idols. Still are. Just seeing a woman of color dominating a sport that didn’t always cater to them historically. Also, their ability at merging sports, fashion, and culture is something that I always was interested in.

How do you get pumped up—or calmed down and focused—before a match?

Usually I try to meditate a little bit—I try to visualize myself just being successful in the match and seeing myself doing successful things. After that, honestly, I’m just on TikTok or just being normal.

Yes—usually some hype rap music, or sometimes some gospel—Kirk Franklin or Tye Tribbett. They don’t really sing; they’re more yelling and stuff, with a choir in the back that’s doing the singing for them. If you’ve ever been to a Black church, it’s kind of that style.

Paris—I just love it. It was the first city that I visited outside of the US, so I think I just always have nostalgia towards that, just realizing how far tennis could take me. It just feels like a home away from home a little bit. People always ask me for Paris recs, but I’m usually just the girl that will just go to the park by herself. I just like the vibe.

Do you have a guilty pleasure or something that you indulge yourself in when you’re away from tennis?

I just started like three weeks ago, but I really enjoy pole dancing. That’s been my thing. I’m one of those people that hobby jumps a lot—I bought a guitar and I haven’t used it; I bought a keyboard—so I need to stop wasting money on stuff that I’m interested in for two weeks. I’m hoping that this one sticks. It’s challenging though—I’m not very great, but one day.

Is there a sport other than tennis that you either watch or play?

I play basketball for fun—I’m not good, I just like it. And I watch American football and basketball—the Dolphins and the Heat—and then track and field when it’s the world championships and the Olympics.

When you’re getting dressed for a big night out, do you have a favorite piece, or something that you love that’s important to you?

I can probably count on my hands the amount of times I’ve went out—in my life—to a bar or club, but I do like to dress up. Sometimes I’ll dress up to just take pictures. Lately I’ve been loving a good heel, because I’m already tall and I just like being taller than people. But also a good bag. I just started working with Miu Miu, so I’ve been experimenting with outfits and stuff with them. They like to do a lot of layering, and layering was something I usually didn’t do because I’m from Florida and it’s hot, but it’s something that I’ve been trying to embrace more. And I love accessories, like a cute bow, or they have the bag charms now—anything to spice up your outfit.

Roland Garros—my first Grand Slam. The whole tournament was just something else. I still remember the emotions to this day. It was the very first tournament where I felt so focused—I could see every little hair on the tennis ball when I was about to hit the shot, and I haven’t ever experienced anything like that before.

Is there a loss this year that was important to you?

Yes—at the US Open when I lost, once again, to Coco Gauff. Coco is a very tough player that I don’t like playing against, but that’s okay. I had two match points—and obviously, with the New York crowd, I get that she would get 95% of the support, and I was actually dealing with that pretty good throughout the whole match. But having two match points to beat her for the first time and to advance to the semifinals of the US Open for the first time—there was a lot on the line that obviously didn’t go my way. That was very, very disappointing for me, and I was sad. But I think with time I’m able to learn from that.

Did you have a hero or a role model when you were growing up?

Yes, my role model was always Roger Federer. To this day I just adore him. For me, he is the best tennis player—not just the way he plays, but what kind of a person he is as well. I just love everything that he does.

I had a very, very quick chat with him at Wimbledon last year. He watched one of my matches and afterward he was there with a lot of other people and I just shook his hand, and he congratulated me and I just said that I’m a big, big fan. But it was very, very, very quick—maybe 20 seconds. But it’s better than nothing, so I’ll take it.

How do you get pumped up—or calmed down and focused—before a match?

I’ve actually been talking to my psychologist about this a lot. She told me that if I felt a little bit more calm before the match, maybe I wouldn’t be as nervous. So I have one routine where I listen to motivating, but calm, music—Billie Eilish, Lana Del Rey. Maybe I read a book for a bit. And then before every match, whether I’m calm or if I need a little bit more energy, I watch Roger Federer’s 2017 Australian Open final against Rafa Nadal—five sets, an amazing match. I don’t know how many times I’ve watched it, but I do it to feel the stress of the match, the adrenaline of how they both played. And when I need a little bit more energy, I play Uno with my team. That’s where things get serious—that’s where we can fight a little bit. I also listen to more energetic music—Bon Jovi, Queen. A little bit of older times, but that’s my taste in music.

If you didn’t play any sport, what kind of job would you have?

I’ve been thinking about this a lot recently. For sure I would study more, go to university—but I also think maybe I might want to try acting. I don’t know—my mom says that I’m a pretty good actor when, like, something happens and I need to make it look like I don’t know what’s going on. Maybe I would just give it a shot and see how I would do.

You don’t seem like a stranger to getting dressed up and going out with your friends—

I love to dress up, and maybe a half a year ago I was manifesting heels, because I had never owned high heels in my life. I was talking about high heels everywhere, in every interview, and then I got a high heel from my agent as a gift. But since then, they’ve just been coming at me. Now I have 10 pairs. I don’t wear a super high heel because otherwise I’d break my leg, but I love to put little heels with everything I wear—with jeans, with pants, with a skirt. I think they make the outfit look a little more elegant, and now, for some reason I like the elegant lifestyle.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/healthcare-dealmakers-chicagos-11b-hospital-merger-another-health-system-asc-deal-and-more" hreflang="en">Healthcare Dealmakers—Chicago's $11B hospital merger, another health system ASC deal and more</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →
Healthcare Dealmakers—Chicago's $11B hospital merger, another health system ASC deal and more" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Healthcare mergers and acquisitions are in no short supply as providers, health tech companies, payers and other industry players look to expand their businesses and gain a competitive edge. Here’s a roundup of new deals that were revealed, closed, rumored or called off during the month of October.

Rush University System for Health and Endeavor Health announced plans to merge, becoming a 12-hospital nonprofit with nearly $11 billion in annual revenue. The resulting entity would command over 400 care sites, employ more than 46,000 people including over 3,400 clinicians, and reach over 2 million Northern Illinois and Northwest Indiana patients per year. The pair are targeting an early 2027 close and outlined plans to commit $1 billion of Endeavor funds toward care in the region.

Private equity firm CD&R and healthcare conglomerate McKesson plan to acquire Option Care Health, a provider of infusion therapy services, in a deal valued at $5.8 billion. The purchase gives equity holders a per-share offer of $32.05 and is expected to close in the first half of 2027. CD&R would be the majority stakeholder in the company, which is the country’s largest independent at-home and alternate site infusion services provider.

Bon Secours Mercy Health announced plans to acquire a majority stake in Surgery Partners, among the country’s largest outpatient surgical care companies, in a take-private deal valuing the acquiree at $5.7 billion. Bain Capital Private Equity will roll over its 39% share of the company as it's purchased by a Bon Secours affiliate at $23.50 per share. Surgery Partners includes more than 200 locations and about 5,000 affiliated physicians across 30 states. The organizations are targeting an early 2027 close.

Risant Health, Kaiser Permanente’s subsidiary value-based care platform, announced Integris Health as its next regional health system acquisition. Financial terms were not disclosed, though the deal aims for an early 2027 close. Integris is Oklahoma’s largest nonprofit provider with 10 acute and 10 specialty hospitals with $3.3 billion in total operating revenues during the 2025 fiscal year.

MultiCare Health System picked up the final needed regulatory approval to absorb Samaritan Health Services during October. The nonprofits then consummated their 18-hospital combination on Nov. 1.

Hackensack Meridian Health has signed a definitive agreement to pick up Central New Jersey’s Hunterdon Health, which would grow the state’s largest health system to 19 total hospitals. The agreement outlines a $200 million capital investment from Hackensack Meridian, plus another $30 million to roll out Epic’s electronic health record.

Cencora (formerly AmerisourceBergen) signed a definitive agreement to acquire American Oncology Network, a collection of more than 350 oncology providers, in a deal valuing the network at $2.4 billion. Cencora said it expects to pay $1.7 billion to increase its 4% interest to 75%.

Parkview Health is acquiring Ovation Healthcare, a nationwide shared services vendor for providers. The 16-hospital Indiana nonprofit did not disclose financial terms, but said it hopes to close the deal within weeks. Parkview views the pickup as an opportunity to boost its administrative capabilities, which could be offered to other rural systems.

Parkview Health also wrapped its acquisition of Goshen Health, a 105-bed hospital with a network of physicians.

Independence Health System, a five-hospital nonprofit in western Pennsylvania, joined the West Virginia University Health System on Oct. 1. The deal was announced late last year and brings a five-year, $800 million capital commitment from WVU Health System toward the Independence hospitals and communities, which will "immediately" begin operating under the WVU Medicine brand.

Kettering Health plans to add Knox Community Hospital as its 15th medical center. The Ohio systems signed a definitive agreement for their deal, which will include undisclosed commitments toward Knox’s facilities, equipment and technology.

Nationwide Children’s Hospital plans to acquire the pediatric practice of Knox Community Hospital in spring 2027. Nationwide plans to combine the practice with one of its affiliates and continue providing services to Knox.

Allegiance Health Management said it plans to divest four Louisiana hospitals: Minden Medical Center, Northern Louisiana Medical Center, Acadian Medical Center and Mercy Regional Medical Center. No potential buyers or deal timelines were given.

Orlando Health closed its acquisition of RMC Health System, an Alabama organization with a 375-bed medical center. The deal was first announced in April, and brings five years of “significant” investment into RMC’s facilities, equipment and technology.

HCA Healthcare sold 31 home health and hospital agencies to Deaconess Associations. The facilities were located across eight states. Financial terms were not disclosed.

MaineHealth added York Hospital to its Southern region, marking the 79-bed facility as the system’s tenth hospital.

Ascension Seton said it is acquiring three locations in the greater Austin area from Family Health Systems, one of which is the Auton-based Family Hospital. The deal is set to close later this year.

Cone Health entered an agreement to acquire three pharmacies in Reidsville, North Carolina: Carolina Apothecary, Belmont Pharmacy and Rx Care.

Humana struck a deal shifting out Welsh, Carson, Anderson & Stowe for BlackRock for equity in a joint venture running about 150 of its CenterWell locations. The deal sees BlackRock buying out WCAS’ equity for about $2.8 billion.

Horizon Blue Cross Blue Shield of New Jersey is acquiring the New Jersey operations of Summit Health, which includes CityMD urgent care sites. Financial details or a potential timeline were not disclosed.

Redesign Health plans to merge with Sheba Medical Center’s innovation arm, ARC Global, creating a roughly $2 billion company. The pair hope to build an end-to-end platform that helps health systems to turn ideas originating with their own clinicians and operators into technology products and standalone companies that can then be scaled across a global network. To help do so, the pair also agreed to acquire strategic advisory and intelligence firm AVIA.

Teladoc Health acquired Teledigm Health, which provides hybrid clinical services and specialized care support for rural and regional health systems, the public virtual care company announced alongside its Q3 earnings. Financial terms were not disclosed. Teladoc leadership said picking up its longtime partner will help it grow with providers serving rural communities.

RadNet, an imaging center operator and seller of radiology technology, is acquiring Onrad, a teleradiology services company with 130 contracted radiologists. The deal is expected to close early next year and will bring Onrad shareholders a collective $350 million plus 5 million RadNet shares, with additional performance-based earnouts.

iRhythm wrapped its acquisition of cardiac remote patient monitoring company VitalConnect for $237.5 million. The deal will help iRhythm evolve from ambulatory cardiac monitoring toward “a broader cardiac monitoring and intelligence platform,” its CEO and president told Fierce Healthcare.

Zocdoc acquired insurance technology company Sohar Health, the first acquisition in its nearly 20-year history and part of its push to evolve beyond an appointment-booking marketplace into broader healthcare access infrastructure. Financial terms were not disclosed.

Carenet Health, which provides patient engagement and navigation tools to over 400 healthcare partners, acquired Orbita, an AI engagement and communication company that does business with providers. Financial terms were not disclosed.

Tegria, a healthcare consultancy and services company, acquired cybersecurity, cloud hosting and managed IT services company CloudWave for an undisclosed sum.

Chapter, a Medicare navigation and retirement advisory company, has acquired Hively, a personalized retirement navigation and membership startup, for an undisclosed sum. The pair intend to launch in-person and online community programming for retirees surrounding retirement and aging topics, a move beyond Medicare navigation alone.

Sagility, a provider and payer technology services company, acquired Chirok Health, which offers clinical documentation improvement, medical coding and revenue integrity services for an undisclosed sum.

Investment firm Sopris Capital said it acquired Blackford Analysis, an AI medical imaging platform, from Bayer for an undisclosed sum. Sopris said it intends to deploy the company’s tools across its healthcare portfolio.

Emergence, a private equity firm, acquired digital intake and online reputation management software company Clinect for an undisclosed sum.

Amazon’s shopping agent now takes orders by text, with Sponsored Product ads in the replies

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Amazon’s shopping agent now takes orders by text message and the thread where those orders get placed will carry ads. Sponsored Products are set to appear inside those conversations, free to advertisers for the time being. Purchases the agent completes on its own will stay ad-free.

The texting option went live today for a small, invite-only group of U.S. customers. Amazon plans to open it to millions more over the coming weeks and anyone keen to jump the queue can sign up for the company’s text updates and wait for an invite.

Once in, customers link a phone number, accept the terms and get a new contact called “Alexa Shopping.” From there they message it the way they’d message a friend. Reorder the laundry detergent from last month. Build a grocery shop from a photo of a recipe. Find a birthday present that arrives by Saturday. Alexa for Shopping searches, compares options and texts back with suggestions, then adds the chosen items to the cart and checks out when told to. Customers who’d rather not be asked can switch on auto-buy and let the agent pull the trigger when a price hits their target.

The ad opportunity sits in the discovery stage of that text exchange. When the agent texts back a set of options, a Sponsored Product can be one of them, which puts brands inside one of the most personal channels on a phone with room for a handful of products at most. Amazon has kept ads to that moment, while the customer is still choosing. Slipping one into a purchase the agent makes is a bigger gamble. After all, no one who asks Alexa to reorder dog food when the price drops wants to find out the bag on the doorstep was the one a brand paid for rather than the one they’d have picked.

Amazon isn’t charging for these placements yet, which gives it a cheap way to test how much advertising people will put up with in a text thread — and there’s no shortage of people to test it on. More than 400 million customers have used Alexa for Shopping in the past 12 months, according to Amazon, and use of its agentic features has grown more than eightfold since January.

“Any new ad placement is generally seen as an interesting test and this approach from Amazon is no different,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Sousa-Rauto. “Amazon are already well known for putting ad slots on the order confirmation page, which whilst generates ad revenue, could be considered non-incremental for most advertisers. As agentic commerce meets advertising, the definition of an impression is critical as well as the reporting and measurement, to understand if it is truly driving efficiency for brands.”

The feature is designed to work across Amazon’s shopping app, website and Alexa-enabled devices, while those text conversations can carry between them. A request started over text carries into the Amazon Shopping app, the site and Alexa-enabled devices and back again. People can send photos of something spotted while scrolling and ask for lookalikes. They can set price alerts or tell the agent to watch for new launches, with Amazon’s own example being a budget when Nike drops new running shoes. The agent also keeps a running tally of preferences. Mention veganism or marathon training once and it shapes the recommendations that follow. Through Shop Direct, it can also surface and buy products from stores beyond Amazon.

“Customers often jot down what they need the moment they think of it in their notes, in their messages, in their group chats,” said Rajiv Mehta, vp of conversational shopping at Amazon. “Now with Alexa for Shopping, your personal shopping agent is just a text away. Message it what you need and Alexa goes to work on your behalf—searching, comparing, and buying so you can get on with your day.”

This text thread is the latest installment of Amazon’s evolving ad business.

In March, Sponsored Products and Brands prompts arrived, followed by Alexa for Shopping, which replaced Rufus in May and took over the main search bar in the app. This was then followed by Alexa+ Agentic Ads which was announced at Cannes Lions this summer, before the company’s advertiser platform was rebranded as Amazon Ads Agent. During the same month, Amazon blocked Meta’s AI shopping agent Muse, stating that the agent accessed its platform without prior notification, failure to properly identify itself and reportedly captured and stored customers’ details.

Nov. 6, 1919: Einstein is right

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The earliest test of Albert Einstein’s general theory of relativity took place just four years after he published it. During the May 29, 1919, total solar eclipse, British astronomer Sir Arthur Eddington and colleagues studied the theory by measuring how the Sun distorts light from background stars.

According to general relativity, any object with mass warps space-time. (The common analogy is that it’s similar to a bowling ball on a trampoline.) So, any passing body will follow a curved trajectory; this is true whether the passing body is an orbiting satellite or a light photon from a background object.

Researchers involved with the 1919 eclipse expedition traveled to two locations to observe totality: northern Brazil and a western island of Africa. According to general relativity, the apparent positions of stars near the Sun would appear to shift by 1.75 arcseconds. The scientists first measured the positions of stars in the Hydra star cluster in early 1919. Eddington and colleagues then recorded the stars’ locations during totality, and compared them to the previous values. They found that indeed, the background stars appeared to shift by roughly the amount general relativity had predicted. (They measured 1.98 arcseconds and 1.61 arcseconds.) The researchers announced their data on Nov. 6, 1919, and Einstein quickly became a household name.

Liz Kruesi is science writer with a background in physics who specializes in astronomy and space-related news.

Airframe Ultra's PS1 cyberpunk city is an amazing tribute to G-Police and WipEout, and I wish you people would slow down to appreciate it

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

But what if we could gawk at the dumpsters

I do not wish to "rule the streets" of Megacity West, dear creators of Airframe Ultra. I wish to be ravished by them, rubbing my face against the rusty corrugated metal of the shipping containers, and savouring the acid blue waters of the bay. I do not want to "become the one thing with freedom in a city of billions: A racer." I want to become the other thing with freedom in a city of billions: a person who slowly drives or – whisper it - walks around just looking at stuff, because gadzooks, this is one of the prettiest PS1 cyberpunk burgs I've laid eyes on.

Those eroded pastel chevrons. Those seductively wobbling vertices. That electric reef of a downtown backdrop, murderously luring your gaze from the on-coming trucks. That layer of invigorating crackle over everything. Every moment or so, I dismount from my hoverbike to inspect some concrete ruin I could swear I last saw in G-Police, or ponder the resemblance between a subway tunnel and WipEout 2097's Gare d'Europa. Then, somebody waps me in the skull with a stop sign at 300 kilometres an hour. But I was already stopped, frantic stranger! Your brutal poetic ironies are wasted on me.

Despite my best efforts, Airframe Ultra is a battle racer. It's co-developed by Videocult, the people behind Rain World – a game it looks nothing like, but is comparable to in the sense that there is an ocean of industrial refuse to navigate, and you will very often die. Each battle race is broken into sprints around a branching course that has been smashed together by the forces of urban decay. You will corkscrew down storm drains and barge through old abandoned clifftop mansions under a scarlet sun, zip along deadly flyovers and thread the barely legible layouts of high density slums.

Your hoverbike handles like a bronco full of helium, and that's before you start wanging it off the gloriously marbled, low-poly rubbish heaps that stud the city, shunting the game's camera through surfaces and exposing the map's underside in what the absolute squares amongst my readers will doubtless deem "an inelegance", but which true turbojunkeys will appreciate for its makeshift exuberance.

You've got a boost button which generally gets you exploded – and all that's before you get to the time-limited battle arenas that bookend each sprint. Here, players attempt to slaughter each other as much as possible, spending collectible cash on floating weapon pick-ups that range from bent pipes to Gatling guns and EMP grenades. Then it's off for another sprint. The first player to earn 10 points and place first in a subsequent sprint wins the match. I have not won many matches. My primary goal right now in Airframe Ultra is persuading everybody to stop and swap some emotes, while I coo over an amazing piece of graffiti I've found.

My efforts to make Airframe Ultra a walking sim aren't just about scenery. The game is surprisingly engrossing on foot, with some woozy tank controls that make me think of the experimental days before every third-person control scheme became a variation on Assassin's Creed, Uncharted or Gears of War. You have to hammer a button to run, for instance, and you'll wind yourself fast. You can also perform punches and kicks in four directions, with grander attacks to discover by smooshing together inputs. You'd best believe I burst into tears when I realised what this reminded me of: it's Bungie's Oni! The game with the finest martial arts system ever coded! There are more obvious influences, like Road Rash, but in these grim days of endless parrying, I'll take all the Konoko callbacks I can get.

Ahhhhh. But if only this had a proper single player mode. Online and local splitscreen with drop-in bot support are all you get. I wouldn't say Airframe is the most balanced deathracer, either. In my experience, and do bear in mind I mostly try to avoid the racing component, victory typically goes to whoever lands a fat cash reserve and grabs an AK47. That mild shambolicness is arguably part of the charm, in that it supplies incentive to stop and drink in the atmosphere. These streets are big and broken and beautiful enough for all of us, friends. Let us rule them together. Read more on Steam.

‘Wild Horse Nine’ Is Part Conspiracy Thriller, Part Buddy Comedy, All Malkovich FTW

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You could say that Chris Carlisle (John Malkovich) is a big deal in certain circles. It’s not like his name was gracing headlines in The New York Times back in the day, or showing up in the Warren Commission Report. But among those in the intelligence community, the man was a dirty-tricks legend. Please allow him to introduce himself, this man of stealth and taste. He was there when Patrice Lumumba was saying his last goodbyes in the Congo, and shouted out “Who killed the Kennedys?” — although having been there when the shots, plural, were fired, his answer to that particular question would be way, way more specific than just “after all, it was you and me.”

This particular devil isn’t looking for sympathy, however. Carlisle has found himself in Chile circa August 1973 for one last gig, helping to set the stage for a coup that will turn the South American country into an authoritarian nightmare for nearly two decades. Now, all he wants is some peace of mind. As well as a final resting place: Carlisle is dying of cancer. So he’s asked his partner in C.I.A.-sponsored crime, Lee Chesterfield (Sam Rockwell), to come with him to Rapa Nui, commonly known as Easter Island. Before he shuffles off this mortal coil, Chris wants to see “the big heads.” Lee wants to grant his friend’s final wish.

There are just two things complicating the endeavor: The older man is losing his memory. And, ironically, he’s also decided to write his memoirs, detailing his long and fruitful career of destabilizing democracies and assassinating dignitaries — including a certain prominent public figure in a Dallas motorcade. That last part doesn’t sit well with the duo’s supervisor, an agency apparatchik named MJ (Steve Buscemi). MJ wants Lee to terminate his pal with extreme prejudice ASAP. Chris, meanwhile, wants Lee to quit complaining about everything and recognize that someone may be out to get them during this little getaway. “When did you die and become the Arch-Pussy Ferdinand?!” he screams. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

An exotic locale, a prickly father-figure-and-son relationship, eloquently profane dialogue that trips gloriously off actors’ tongues — you’ve probably guessed that playwright turned filmmaker Martin McDonagh is the person spinning this yarn. Wild Horse Nine follows a similar template to the writer-director’s other screen work, specifically his feature debut, In Bruges, (2008) and his most recent offering, The Banshees of Inisherin (2022). This new film falls somewhere between those two in terms of full metal McDonagh-mania. It brims with a maturity and confidence that puts it a few notches above the tale of Colin Farrell and Brendan Gleason’s hit men slouching through Belgium; and isn’t quite the stone-cold masterpiece that the same duo’s bloody bromance break-up in West Ireland is. But McDonagh’s latest is a great reminder that if a successful formula ain’t broke, it doesn’t require fixing, or even light tweaking. In the right hands, there’s a hell of a lot that can be done with two men, some first-rate banter, and a historical flashpoint that eerily resonates with our current need for a reckoning about American adventures abroad.

Whether or not you preternaturally dig McDonagh’s penchant for gallows humor, envelope-pushing notions of propriety, and ocean-water-level saltiness will be a breaking point here, of course; his work isn’t without harsh critics or controversy. But this daffy mix of buddy comedy and conspiracy thriller benefits from his facility with dialogue that’s just south of screwball, as well as the mutual admiration society between McDonagh and actors. Rockwell, essentially a member of the director’s rep company — it’s technically their fourth collaboration, if you count treading the boards — seems to inherently get the rhythm of McDonagh’s bickering back-and-forths. Cast as the foul-mouthed voice of reason instead of the volatile guy for once, he still manages to feel like a wild card. “Live wire” is Rockwell’s comfort zone, and you can feel the crackle in his exchanges with Buscemi, once again wearing perpetual irritation like a velvet cape, and Parker Posey as Lee’s needling-is-my-love-language wife. Mariana di Girolamo and Ailín Salas enter the picture as two Chilean students who cross Chris and Lee’s path, with Di Girolamo acting as both the audience’s aghast surrogate and the movie’s conscience. Related Content How Do You Remake a Horror Classic? Ask Mike Flanagan How to Pick Pockets (and Steal Scenes) With John Turturro She Cries. She Rages. She Sings Harry Styles. Is Sandra Hüller the MVP of 2026? The Dream of the Nineties Animation Boom Is Alive in ‘Wildwood’

But let’s not pretend this two-hander-with-benefits doesn’t offer one star a sturdier showcase than everyone else. Wild Horse Nine lives or dies by the Mutt & Jeff chemistry of its leads, both of whom keep the proceedings propped up in between plot points and temporary pacing lulls. But this is a John Malkovich for-the-win affair. And his interpretation of Chris — sometimes cantankerous, sometimes childlike, sometimes flaky and forgetful, forever suffused with a metastasizing sense of guilt over his past misdeeds — is what makes the film draw blood even in its sillier, more surreal moments. Not just because the longtime C.I.A. spook is facing down a dwindling future or losing his grip on the present. It’s more that the ghosts of dirty-tricks past aren’t done with him. You’re not sure if disease, an agency lackey’s bullet, or a toxic amount of remorse is the thing that will finally put him six feet under.

There are any number of exchanges or oddly poignant asides (see: Malkovich nuzzling with a horse, who “smells” his cancer) or silent expressions that the veteran actor forms in Wild Horse Nine that would make a greatest-hits reel. We’d cherry-pick Chris’ brief phone call with his estranged brother, played by Tom Waits (!). Trading barbs while Waits lounges around Monument Valley — because if you’re going to take the U.S. to task, why not lean into it and go whole-hog in John Ford country? — his sibling notes that once the powers that be took down the golden boy from Hyannis Port, the whole country went to shit. “The whole country was already shit,” Chris replies. “You just didn’t know it.” In journalistic terms, this is called burying the lede. In Malkovich’s weary reading, however, it feels someone trying to shoulder the 20th century’s legacy of geopolitical fuckitude while being crushed underneath its 20-ton bulk. Trending Stories Appeals Court Says Lawsuit Over ‘The Pitt’ Can Proceed, Cites ‘Numerous’ Similarities to ‘ER’ Adam Lambert on His Future With Queen, New Music, and Why the Show Must Go On Pink Floyd Marks ‘Animals’ 50th Anniversary With Reissue Featuring Unreleased Music As Nicki Minaj Rallied for Trump's GOP, Her Lawyers Told Court She Still Owes Them $295,000

Wild Horse Nine is a funny movie, even when — especially when — McDonagh slows things down and just lets Rockwell and Malkovich cook in tandem. It’s also a rage-filled movie, in which all that ha-ha bang-bang banter is the only thing keeping the bile from rising in McDonagh’s throat. At its core, it’s another bromance fable, revolving around the familial love between two men that’s thicker than spilled blood, and the only real thing in a world of fake global-policing shenanigans. You can easily bask in the joy of watching the actors trade verbal rabbit punches. But peer just past the surface, and you’ll see a screed on the paranoid style of American politics, in which the filmmaker demands that memories of anti-democratic enablement are not permanently erased from the collective consciousness. Guilt and regret are killers. They’re also the only rational responses to a long history of violence.

‘Buena Vista Social Club’ Sets Broadway Closing; West End Production Announced For 2027

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Buena Vista Social Club, the Tony-winning musical inspired by the legendary Cuban artists that give the show its title, will close on Broadway January 17, 2027, after a 96-week, 767-performance run.

Producers announced the closing date today. Although the production has not recouped its reported $17 million investment, they say it is “on schedule” to do so.

The musical, which began previews on February 21, 2025, at the Gerald Schoenfeld Theatre, opened March 19 of that year to good reviews and consistently strong box office, features a book by Marco Ramirez, direction by Saheem Ali, choreography by Patricia Delgado and Justin Peck, creative consultation by David Yazbek, music supervision by Dean Sharenow and orchestrations, arrangements and music direction by Marco Paguia.

“From our very first trip to Cuba to meet the musicians who created this masterpiece, I knew we had something truly special, something to cherish,” said lead producer Orin Wolf in a statement. “I am in awe of what Marco R., Saheem, Patricia, Justin, David, Marco P. and Dean have created artistically, bringing this extraordinary music to life on stage and presenting the first Broadway score performed exclusively in Spanish. Watch on Deadline

“I am deeply grateful for the time we’ve had on Broadway – and the speed with which the team has created three more productions,” he continued. “We are thrilled the Broadway production is on schedule to recoup as we look ahead to an exciting tour, special engagements in Mexico and Puerto Rico, the film and so much more.”

A West End production in London is scheduled to begin in 2027. As of now, there are four productions of the musical up and running: On Broadway; on a North American tour (currently at the Music Hall at Fair Park in Dallas, Texas; at the Antonio Paoli Festival Hall at the Centro de Bellas Artes in Puerto Rico, and at the Teatro de los Insurgentes in Mexico City.

All feature the first Broadway score performed solely in Spanish. Producers say the musical has attracted the highest number of Hispanic ticket buyers in the history of The Shubert Organization.

The Broadway production scored five Tony Awards including Best Performance by an Actress in a Featured Role in a Musical (Natalie Venetia Belcon); Best Choreography; Best Orchestrations; Best Sound Design of a Musical; and a Special Tony Award for the 10-piece center-stage band. The cast album won the Grammy Award. (Belcon is returning to the production November 30, as is Ashley De La Rosa on November 16).

While the production has remained one of the stronger Broadway shows, the weekly tallies have dimmed considerably since its peak performances last year, when the musical often grossed well above the $1 million mark. The most recent available figure, for the week ending November 1, was $678,878, with attendance at 89% of the Schoenfeld’s capacity.

The current BVSC cast oincludes Gina Torres, Rick Negron, Mel Semé, Jainardo Batista Sterling, Isa Antonetti, Da’Von T. Moody, Wesley Wray, Jesús Pupo, Renesito Avich, Tanairi Sade Vazquez, Chris Myers, José “Candelita” Iglesias, Bernie Williams (Guitar), Angélica Beliard, Joshua Gonzalez, Jacob Guzman, Ian Lassiter, Héctor Juan Maisonet, Ilda Mason, Marielys Molina, Eric Parra, Sophia Ramos, Clinton Roane and Anthony Santos.

Inspired by true events, the musical tells the inspiring story of the legendary Afro-Cuban Havana band who reunited to record the 1997 Grammy Award-winning album Buena Vista Social Club.

BVSC is produced on Broadway by Orin Wolf, John Styles, Barbara Broccoli, Atlantic Theater Company, Luis Miranda, LaChanze and John Leguizamo, with Executive Producer Allan Williams.

‘Stranger Things’ Live-Action Prequel ‘Other Worlds’ A Go At Netflix From ‘First Shadow’ Team Of Kate Trefry & Duffer Brothers

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The long-rumored live-action Stranger Things spinoff series has been officially revealed by Netflix. Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure, co-created by Kate Trefry and The Duffer Brothers and directed by Joe Wright, is a prequel set in the 1950s, before Stranger Things and before the stage play Stranger Things: The First Shadow. It will premiere in 2028.

A teaser, which you can watch below, provides more information about the offshoot’s setting — both in terms of time, April 19, 1955, and location, Marion, Nevada. It features a deserted bike by a restricted area at night, with town lights in the distance and an ominous disturbance in the sky that resembles a wormhole creation. Related Stories Casting Maya Hawke Boards 'Magic: The Gathering' Movie From Legendary And Hasbro News Ted Sarandos Has No Regrets Over WB Pursuit, Talks Netflix's Engagement Growth Challenges, Overall Deals & Casey Bloys

The spinoff has been several years in the making, with Matt and Ross Duffer acknowledging that they’d had the idea since Season 4 of the mothership series came out in the summer of 2022. “We are moving forward with it. Netflix, they finally know what it is,” Matt Duffer told Deadline last November. Watch on Deadline

And while the creative auspices of Other Worlds are all stressing that it will feature “a wholly new story,” “new characters, themes and mythology,” its connection to the Tony-winning The First Shadow is undeniable. Longtime Stranger Things writer Trefry wrote the stage play from a story by her, the Duffer Brothers and Jack Thorne.

The new series is set in Nevada and takes place just a couple of years before the events of the Nevada experiment in The First Shadow. In an interview with Variety last November, Matt Duffer revealed that the spinoff will explain the rock young Henry Creel finds in a scientist’s briefcase in the Nevadan caves, which was first introduced in The First Shadow before it was featured in Season 5 of Stranger Things, adding that the new series “will answer some of the loose threads that are remaining.”

The song in the teaser, “Sh-Boom” by The Chords, also is related to young Henry. It’s playing in Season 5, Episode 4 of Stranger Things when Max visits the memory of Henry Creel in school at Hawkins High, a setting that also originated in The First Shadow.

And while Marion, Nevada does not appear connected to the play, which is set in Rachel, Lincoln County, Nevada in addition to Hawkins, Indiana, that may change.

Netflix filmed multiple performances of the The First Shadow in February. A theatergoer who attended one of the tapings having watched the play several times prior, shared their confusion over the location being changed to Marion, Nevada for the proshot. The TV version of The First Shadow, which is said to include tweaks aligning the play better with Stranger Things Season 5, has not been scheduled for release yet but Netflix will likely use it to bridge the mothership series and the prequel.

A February taping of The First Shadow featuring Marion, NV would also illustrate how long the prequel has been gearing up. By September, word was that the project, at the time widely rumored to be set in the world of The First Shadow and take place in the 1950s, was said to be nearing greenlight for a 2027 start of production. Netflix would not confirm the pickup, holding off to make the announcement on Stranger Things day, Nov. 6, when the original series’ Will Byers disappeared.

Trefry serves as writer and showrunner on Other Worlds. While the Duffer Brothers co-created and executive produce the series with her, the duo were never going to be day-to-day showrunners on the new series as they are under an exclusive overall deal at Paramount. (Their executive services on any Stranger Things projects via their Upside Down Pictures are carved out of the pact.)

“We’ve been dreaming of Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure for many years now, and to see it become a reality is surreal and exhilarating,” Matt & Ross Duffer said. “We’re so grateful to our incredible team of collaborators: our showrunner Kate Trefry, who has been in the trenches with us since Season 2 and wrote Stranger Things: The First Shadow; and our director, Joe Wright, a brilliant artist whose work we’ve long admired. We can’t wait for you to see what they’re cooking up. It’s Stranger Things, but also thrillingly fresh, with a new setting, new heroes and villains, and new… actually, we’re not allowed to say any more. Now, on to casting. In the meantime — watch the skies, nerds!”

Stranger Things executive producer/director Shawn Levy also recently left Netflix for an overall deal at Disney’s 20th Television. Like the Duffers, he is executive producing Other Worlds though his 21Laps Entertainment but is not directing. Wright (Pride & Prejudice, Atonement) is set to direct the first three episodes and executive produce the new series. Paul Dichter, Shelley Meals, Andrea Sperling and Pavlina Hatoupis also executive produce. “Stranger Things has always belonged to the fans, and their passion made it a worldwide phenomenon,” said Jinny Howe, Netflix Head of Scripted Series, US & Canada. “Today, on Stranger Things Day, we’re honored to partner again with Matt and Ross Duffer and Kate Trefry to give fans a new dimension of this universe to step into. Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure takes us to the 1950s with new heroes and all the heart, mystery, and spectacle of the original series. We’re proud to keep building this world with the Duffers, Kate, Shawn Levy, and Joe Wright, and we can’t wait for fans to see what’s next.”

This is the latest “first” for Trefry that stems from Stranger Things: she landed her first staff writer job on the mothership series, wrote her first play with The First Shadow and has now created her first series with Other Worlds.

“The Duffer Brothers gave me my first paying job in Hollywood, and it has been a joy and a fever dream ever since,” Trefry said. “Now, it is my insane honor and privilege to be bringing this new chapter of Stranger Things to life alongside them and our incredible director Joe Wright. With the support of Netflix and 21Laps, we have had the time of our lives returning to all the horror, wonder and mystery of the flagship, while also telling a wholly new story, one we hope will blow your minds and break your hearts all over again. There are so many more mysteries to explore. Welcome to Other Worlds.”

Created by The Duffer Brothers, Stranger Things debuted in 2016 and became Netflix’s first owned hit series, a global phenomenon with over 1.2 billion views across Seasons 1–4.

“All of us at 21Laps are thrilled and privileged to be continuing the storytelling legacy of Stranger Things,” Levy said. “With Other Worlds, the Duffer Brothers and Kate Trefry craft a new ensemble and adventure that is imbued with the DNA of our flagship series while exploring a new era, setting and all-new characters, themes and mythology — the seeds of which may have been planted in Hawkins, but now venture into other worlds of spectacle, action and emotion.”

Other Worlds marks the second Stranger Things TV spinoff and third overall, including stage play The First Shadow. The animated Stranger Things: Tales From ’85, which fills the gap between Seasons 2 and 3 of the original series, has released two season and is awaiting a renewal decision.

“Whatever we do next, I’ll tell you this, it’s not going to be set in the ’80s. I think we’re done with the ’80s,” Ross told Deadline last November, with Matt jokingly adding, “Kids on bikes. Probably done with that.”

Judging by the teaser for Other Worlds, bikes may be back in the picture.

Katie Campione contributed to this report.

Jack Whitehall, Nina Dobrev, Greg Kinnear & Andie MacDowell Set For Rom-Com ‘Honeymooners’; HanWay & CAA Launch For AFM

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Jack Whitehall (Malice), Nina Dobrev (The Vampire Diaries), Oscar nominee Greg Kinnear (As Good As It Gets) and Golden Globe nominee Andie MacDowell (Four Weddings And A Funeral) are set to star in rom-com Honeymooners.

Hanway Films will be launching international sales at the AFM for filmmaker Daryl Wein’s (Something From Tiffany’s) feature which is described as “a funny intergenerational story about second chances and finding love where you least expect it”.

CAA Media Finance is on board for North American sales. The film is produced under Wein’s Explosive Pictures banner. Related Stories News International Insider: Russell Brand Guilty; AFM Fires Up; Trudie Styler Interview News Letitia Wright To Star In Supernatural Horror 'In Her Shadow' For 'Obsession' Producer & Bankside -- AFM

Due to shoot in Italy in the first quarter of 2027, the film’s synopsis reads: “Modern Londoners Freddy (Jack Whitehall) and Cleo (Nina Dobrev) have just secretly divorced, convinced they’re better off as best friends. But their amicable split takes an unexpected turn when Freddy’s parents, Marty (Greg Kinnear) and Julie (Andie MacDowell), and Cleo’s mother, Rose, surprise them with the honeymoon of their dreams in Italy. The catch? The whole family is coming along. As the family descends on an idyllic vineyard outside Rome, long-buried secrets, unexpected attractions, and increasingly tangled loyalties threaten to unravel the entire holiday.” Watch on Deadline

Daryl Wein said: “With Honeymooners, I wanted to make a romantic comedy that feels funny, heartfelt, surprising, and deeply human. It’s about the messy, unpredictable nature of love at every age, and the absurd lengths we go to hold onto it, set against the breathtaking backdrop of Italy. My hope is to give audiences a joyful cinematic escape that feels both fresh and timeless.”

Gabrielle Stewart, CEO HanWay Films said: “It is hard not to feel seduced by Daryl’s vision for Honeymooners. It combines the wit and charm of some of our beloved British rom-coms with the gloss and unadulterated joy of Hollywood comedies, set in some of the most beautiful locations in Italy. This and the brilliant multigenerational cast makes it a film for absolutely everyone.”

Jack Whitehall is represented by CAA and Independent Talent Group; Nina Dobrev is represented by CAA and Lighthouse Management + Media; Greg Kinnear is represented by CAA and Anonymous Content; Andie MacDowell is represented by Gersh and manager Risa Shapiro; Daryl Wein is represented by Dalip Sethi at Rain and Jackoway Austen Tyerman.

Daily Briefing: Liverpool, Man Utd and Chelsea all scouting Milan centre-back

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

AC Milan defender Koni De Winter is attracting interest from three top clubs in the Premier League, according to a report.

De Winter had a night to forget last night as he was among the worst performers in Milan’s 2-0 defeat to Ferencvaros. However, just a few nights prior he kept Marcus Thuram under wraps in the derby win over Inter, showcasing the levels he is capable of.

Performances like that seem to have attracted interest from clubs around Europe. The Daily Briefing are reporting that Liverpool, Manchester United, and Chelsea are ‘all among the main suitors’ for the Belgian defender.

WME Signs BLACKPINK’s JISOO For Worldwide Representation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The K-pop star joins WME just days after the release of "Addicted," the second pre-release single from her forthcoming debut solo album.

BLACKPINK‘s JISOO has signed with WME (William Morris Endeavor) for worldwide representation.

Announced Friday (Nov. 6), the K-pop superstar joins the WME roster just days after the release of “Addicted,” the second pre-release single from her forthcoming debut solo album. Earlier, in September, she dropped the single “Click,” and a third solo song is expected in the weeks ahead, “Sun Showers,” an original number that will appear on the The Angry Birds Movie 3 soundtrack, according to The Hollywood Reporter.

As a member of the record-busting BLACKPINK, JISOO completed the sold-out Deadline World Tour earlier this year, a trek that stopped in major stadiums across North America, Europe and Asia. Related Kenny MacPherson, Veteran Music Publisher and Big Deal Music Co-Founder, Dies at 71 Jessica Lynch

Adjacent to her pop career, the in-demand South Korean artist has a growing profile in film and fashion. She’s the global ambassador for Dior, Cartier, Tommy Hilfiger and Salomon; was appointed the face of ALO Yoga, and in 2024 became the first musician to be the face of the London fashion house Self-Portrait; she landed a starring role in the Netflix romcom Boyfriend on Demand, which premiered in March; and recently launched brand collaborations with Off-White and Pokémon.

It’s been full speed ahead for JISOO, who teased her new solo chapter last year with “EYES CLOSED,” a collaboration with ex-One Direction star Zayn. The single reached No. 72 on the Billboard Hot 100. With that effort, BLACKPINK became the first girl group to have every member enter the Hot 100 as a solo artist.

BLACKPINK released the DEADLINE EP in February of this year, after completing the tour of the same name. DEADLINE opened at No. 8 on the Billboard 200, and marked their first major music release since hitting No. 1 on the all-genres albums tally with 2022’s Born Pink.

JISOO began her solo journey in 2023, with the release of her debut EP, ME, which became the first album by a female solo artist in South Korea to surpass one million sales. Its single, “꽃(FLOWER),” powered to No. 2 on the Billboard Global 200 in April of that year, and No. 1 on the Global Excl. US chart. A debut mini album arrived in 2025, AMORTAGE, and included songs “earthquake” and “Your Love.

When her album proper arrives, expect the full package. “Since I’m preparing a full-length album this time, I’m working on showing a wider range of music,” she told V Magazine for an interview published in May. “I think I’d be really happy if people listen to this album and say they discovered a new side of me.”

At WME, JISOO joins a stable of artists that includes Adele, Andrea Bocelli, Billie Eilish, Bob Dylan, Bruno Mars, Chris Stapleton, Coldplay, Depeche Mode, Foo Fighters and many others.
