Teva's 3 Key Branded Drugs Grew Sales 33% Last Quarter. Is This Turnaround Stock a Buy Right Now?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Teva's three branded therapies of Austedo, Ajovy, and Uzedy brought in $1.1 billion in third-quarter revenue.

Teva Pharmaceuticals (TEVA -0.17%) has increased annual revenue for three consecutive years, following a stretch in which annual revenue fell for six consecutive years.

The culprits for the Israel-based pharmaceutical company's six-year slide were increased competition from generics, pricing pressures, and the loss of exclusivity for key products. Thanks to an emphasis on higher-margin therapies, the company appears to be back on track. Its shares are up more than 34% so far this year.

The road back may still be a bumpy one. In the third quarter, revenue was $4.4 billion, down 2% year over year. However, there are plenty of positive signs.

The company's pivot-to-growth strategy involves moving toward more innovative brand-name treatments and complex biologics, away from the company's former bread-and-butter of generics.

Combined sales across its three main innovative growth drivers, including tardive dyskinesia treatment Austedo, migraine therapy Ajovy, and schizophrenia treatment Uzedy, rose 33%, year over year, to $1.1 billion for the quarter. That helped offset the company's generics sales of $2.16 billion, down 16% from the same quarter a year ago.

The new drugs helped Teva improve gross profit margin from 51.4% in the same period a year ago to 55.5%, while non-GAAP gross profit rose by 345 basis points to 58.7%, the highest it has been in a decade.

The company is in the process of launching another promising branded therapy, Weltruza, a once-monthly subcutaneous injectable for adults with schizophrenia. It was approved on Oct. 9 by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA). The company estimates there are roughly 2.2 million people in the U.S. with undiagnosed schizophrenia, with an annual sales potential of $1.5 billion to 2 billion.

Ecopipam, a treatment for pediatric Tourette syndrome, is expected to be approved in the first half of 2027. It affects nearly 100,000 worldwide. The company is also hoping to launch the DARI asthma inhaler as early as next year.

Teva completed a major equity milestone by terminating its legacy American Depositary Shares (ADS) program and transitioning to direct trading of ordinary shares on the NYSE on Sept. 14. This conversion removed extra depositary fees for investors and streamlined foreign tax handling, bringing Teva into line with standard U.S. corporate equity structures.

Teva is dual-listed on the Tel Aviv Stock Exchange, but the NYSE serves as its primary market liquidity engine. The NYSE listing gives U.S. institutional funds, active asset managers, and retail investors direct, frictionless access to trade the equity in U.S. dollars.

Maintaining high visibility on the world's largest stock exchange reinforces the company's identity as a global biopharmaceutical leader rather than just a regional domestic player.

Teva reached its original 2027 target of $800 million in annual biosimilar revenue a year early, in 2026. Expanded partnerships, including its recently announced commercialization agreement with Samsung Bioepis for biosimilars to Remicade and Enbrel, are establishing a durable secondary pipeline to backstop generic erosion.

The company spent $297 million on research and development in the third quarter, up 16% year over year, with the payoff being a more robust pipeline of biosimilars, as well as immunology and neuroscience therapies.

The company has 15 biosimilars in its pipeline and says it has the potential to develop more than 31 by 2030.

Since 2019, Teva has paid down roughly $12 billion in total debt, a hangover from legacy acquisitions and legal settlements, driving toward its target leverage ratio of 2.0x net debt-to-earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) by the end of 2027.

Over the past decade, it has decreased quarterly net debt by 61%.

Major rating agencies (Moody's, S&P, and Fitch) upgraded Teva back to investment-grade status, enabling the company to successfully refinance $4.9 billion of debt on more favorable terms.

The turnaround is not without headwinds. U.S. generic revenues dropped 39% year-over-year to $718 million in the quarter due to intense market competition.

The company is on track to see a slight decline in revenue this year. It also saw earnings per share (EPS) fall 47% year-over-year to $0.20 in the third quarter.

Maintaining growth depends entirely on the continued commercial ramp of its innovative portfolio, supplemented by recent product approvals such as Weltruza to offset the natural drag of legacy commoditized generics.

However, the company appears headed in the right direction and, despite its rise this year, remains a bargain, trading at less than 21 times forward earnings.

Eli Lilly is partnering with Hims & Hers to sell its weight loss drugs

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The agreement covers Zepbound and Foundayo for patients who pay out of pocket through the telehealth platform

Eli Lilly $LLY announced an agreement on Friday with Hims & Hers to offer its weight loss drugs Zepbound and Foundayo through the telehealth platform. The deal gives eligible patients who get a prescription from a licensed provider on the Hims & Hers platform a path to the medicines at a self-pay price, Lilly said.

Zepbound (tirzepatide) is a weekly injection, and Foundayo (orforglipron) is a daily tablet. Both are FDA-approved. Hims & Hers posted the announcement in its newsroom the same day.

Hims & Hers stock climbed 6.3% before the market opened Friday, while Lilly stock slipped 1.4%.

Foundayo is priced at $149 monthly for its lowest dose, and Zepbound begins at $299 monthly, according to Bloomberg.

Weight loss patients on Hims & Hers receive a membership that includes 24/7 access to a care team, nutrition guidance, regular clinical check-ins and access to the company's weight loss community, according to the Lilly release.

Ilya Yuffa, president of Lilly USA and Global Customer Capabilities, told Bloomberg that the company wants to meet consumers where they are. "Our goal is to expand access as much as possible in the different channels, knowing where consumers are and meeting them where they are," he said. Yuffa also told Bloomberg that patients on Hims & Hers previously had to go elsewhere to obtain Lilly's drugs, an extra step that deterred many of them from beginning treatment.

"This is where customers are wanting care and expecting care," Hims & Hers CEO Andrew Dudum told Bloomberg. Dudum anticipates a continued collaboration as Lilly's product lineup grows, including with the obesity drug retatrutide, according to Bloomberg.

Lilly had earlier kept its distance from Hims & Hers over compounded weight loss treatments, according to Bloomberg. Hims & Hers has never offered compounded copies of Lilly's medicines and pledged to keep it that way, and Yuffa told Bloomberg that commitment weighed heavily when Lilly made its choice.

Hims & Hers unveiled a comparable arrangement with Novo Nordisk earlier in the year, according to Bloomberg. Lilly also has existing arrangements with telehealth providers such as Ro and WeightWatchers.

In its forward-looking statement, Hims & Hers cited the timing and logistics of integrating and rolling out Lilly medications on its platform as a risk, along with its ability to perform under the agreement on pricing and fulfillment.

Brookfield Asset Management posted fee-related earnings up 15% as it raised $25 billion

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The asset manager raised $123 billion in the first nine months of 2026, and fee-bearing capital grew 13% to $682 billion

Brookfield Asset Management reported fee-related earnings of $864 million for the third quarter, up 15% from a year earlier, as it raised $25 billion from investors.

Distributable earnings climbed by 15%, reaching $763 million, which works out to 47 cents for each share. Net income was $3.0 billion, compared with $692 million a year earlier, and included a $2.0 billion non-cash gain on the company's acquisition and consolidation of Oaktree.

Year-to-date fundraising reached $123 billion. Fee-bearing capital grew 13% from a year earlier to $682 billion, the company said.

"Our fundraising is showing no signs of slowing down and is indicative of the record-level of activity we are seeing across our business," Connor Teskey, CEO of Brookfield Asset Management, said in a statement.

The infrastructure group brought in $7 billion over the three months, of which $3.4 billion went to its flagship strategy. Once the quarter closed, it secured an additional $13 billion in commitments to that strategy, lifting the cumulative amount raised so far to $26 billion. The company anticipates completing the fund's first close within the next few weeks.

The company’s AI infrastructure fund and infrastructure debt strategy received $10 billion in commitments during the past year. Brookfield signed agreements to build and run an AI factory in the U.K. and to put up to $9 billion into Korean computing capacity for artificial intelligence through partnerships with Naver and Nvidia $NVDA. The credit business raised $12 billion in the quarter.

Distributable earnings per share came in at the consensus forecast of six analysts polled by Zacks Investment Research, according to The Associated Press. Four analysts who responded to a Zacks poll had forecast adjusted revenue of $1.61 billion. The company reported fee revenues of $1.62 billion.

Brookfield stock was trading higher before the market opened on Friday. The company will hold a conference call at 10 a.m. ET on Friday. Its board declared a quarterly dividend of $0.5025 a share, payable on Dec. 31 to shareholders of record as of Nov. 30.

In July, Brookfield announced a partnership with NextEra $NEE Energy to develop a $100 billion data center campus at the U.S. Department of Energy's Paducah Site in western Kentucky. The project remains subject to the negotiation and execution of definitive documentation.

Bruce Flatt, CEO of parent company Brookfield Corp., said in a statement at the time that the site “will be the seed of our plan to invest $100 billion in AI infrastructure.”

Lol: Beond Airlines & Air Cairo Plan All-Business Class Carrier In Egypt

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Guys, sometimes I wonder if I’m losing my mind, or if others are. All-business class carrier Beond Airlines has just announced that it’s partnering with all-economy class carrier Air Cairo to launch an all-business class airline flying to and from Egypt.

No disrespect to Egypt, but of all the business ideas in the world, is this really the one that makes the most sense? Maybe next we’ll see Beond partner with Ryanair to offer all-business class service between Leeds and Agadir?

Beond Airlines and Air Cairo have announced a partnership to bring Beond’s all-business class travel experience to Egypt. The two airlines signed a memorandum of understanding for the concept.

For the summer of 2027, Air Cairo will operate Beond-branded flights with all-business class configurations, connecting Egypt with Europe and the Gulf region. This is clearly targeted at inbound tourism to Egypt, so “travellers reach Egypt’s coast, its history and its cities rested and at ease.”

Routes and schedules will be announced in the coming months, with bookings expected to open in the first half of 2027. Just so we’re on the same page here, Air Cairo operates a fleet of planes exclusively in an all-economy configuration, so the airline hasn’t seen merit to offering even a small business class cabin. But now they’ve decided “hey, what we really need is a plane that exclusively has business class?”

Here’s what Beond CEO Tero Taskila had to say about this concept:

“Egypt has some of the most remarkable places on earth, and many travellers who want to reach them in comfort. Air Cairo knows this market better than anyone. Together we can start the holiday from home, not at the hotel. Our cabin gives us the luxurious surroundings. Our people give us the experience. Both have to match the places we fly to.”

Meanwhile here’s what Egypt’s Minister of Tourism and Antiquities, Sherif Fathy, had to say about this:

“Encouraging this partnership is an integral part of the Ministry’s strategy to promote Egypt’s unparalleled tourism diversity and expand its range of distinctive tourism products and experiences. We highly value the role of Air Cairo as one of the key supporters of the tourism industry, whose efforts contribute to enhancing connectivity, supporting tourism growth, and strengthening Egypt’s position as a leading global tourism destination. Such partnerships reflect our commitment to fostering closer cooperation with our partners in the aviation sector to achieve sustainable growth and further enhance the competitiveness of the Egyptian tourism destination.”

Beond Airlines was initially formed as an airline intended to transport people to and from the Maldives. I’ve flown the airline, and the onboard experience was actually quite lovely (with two caveats — the lack of Wi-Fi, plus the limited range of the planes requiring a refueling stop, which limits the ability to get rest).

This airline obviously has funding, and the company’s CEO is a really nice guy who has a fair bit of experience. But nothing about the past few years gives me any faith in the future of the company.

When Beond Airlines launched in 2023, management claimed the airline would have 32 planes and serve 60 destinations within five years, which would be by 2028. Currently, the airline still has two planes, with no additional firm orders, at least as far as I know.

It has just been announcement after announcement from Beond, with little substance to anything:

I have no clue what’s going on here anymore, though I’d assume it’s one of two things:

But I have to say, of everything we’ve seen from Beond, I find this to possibly be the most bizarre development yet. Egypt is generally a budget vacation destination, and not a premium vacation destination. So if an all-business class experience to the Maldives didn’t prove successful, I can’t imagine that Egypt is the key to unlocking prosperity for the airline.

Beond Airlines and Air Cairo are partnering to launch an all-business class airline that will serve Egypt. Egypt is generally a budget travel destination, and there’s an irony to an all-economy airline getting involved in an all-business class operation.

What can anyone really say at this point, though? Beond is all over the place in terms of its strategy. Initially it was going to fly to dozens of destinations out of the Maldives. Then it promised to set up subsidiaries all over the place, including partnering with an airline that liquidated just weeks later. Then the airline suspended commercial operations altogether. And now the airline is planning all-business class service in Egypt.

What do you make of Beond’s latest Egypt play?

Trump Is Gonna Cave On Offshore Wind In 5…4…3…

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

…JK! US President Donald Trump will never cry surrender in the war against offshore wind turbines. The President was burned once before, when a wind farm arose in Aberdeen Bay in Scotland, within view of a Trump-branded golf course back in the early 2000’s. Never again! The President has taken extreme measures to prevent such blight from tainting the shores of the United States. Nevertheless, scores of the pesky oversized pinwheels have already eluded the Trump chopper, and many more are on the way.

The latest news concerns CVOW, the 2.6-gigwatt Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project under the wing of the leading utility Dominion. As the name of the project indicates, CVOW sits in the Atlantic Ocean about 24 nautical miles (28 land miles) off the coast of Virginia Beach, Virginia.

CVOW began taking shape during Trump’s first turn around the Oval Office, and construction was well under way when his second term began on January 20, 2025.

Immediately upon taking office, Trump abruptly suspended the offshore wind lease program administered by the Department of the Interior. He also ordered spurious re-reviews of offshore projects that were already in the permitting pipeline, and he had the Department of Justice administer a legally questionable buy-back program to prevent activity from stirring among other lease holders. In addition, he had the Interior Department issue multiple emergency orders aimed at stopping five offshore projects off the coasts of Rhode Island, Connecticut, New Jersey, New York, Maryland, and Massachusetts, where construction work was already well under way.

So far, though, only one part of the effort has stuck. Last year a federal judge affirmed that any President, Trump or not, has the authority to stop issuing new offshore leases. However, the same judge decided there is no blanket authority to suspend offshore leases that are already in the permitting process. The buy-back program is also in litigation, with critics raising questions about the funds used to pay off lease holders among other issues.

Additionally,  the Interior Department failed to convince multiple federal judges that the emergency orders had any merit. Work has continued on all five Atlantic coast projects, including CVOW.

CVOW began delivering its first kilowatts to shore in March, and Dominion recapped its progress on the wind farm in its Q3 report on October 30. As handily summarized by the news organization Maritime Executive, the project currently clocks in at 85% complete. All of the main turbine components — nacelles, towers, and blades — for the wind farm’s 176 offshore wind turbines have been fabricated. Forty-four have been fully installed so far. With a 45th coming any day now, the wind farm currently hosts 640 megawatts out of the 2,600 megawatt total.

Since Trump declared an “Energy Emergency” in January of 2025, it’s fair to ask why he has spent so much time, energy and taxpayer dollars to crush an entire domestic energy industry. After all, if one was not reading too closely between the lines of the declaration, the new energy policy is little more than a rehash of former President Barack Obama’s “All Of The Above” energy policy. As further embellished by Energy Secretary Chris Wright, the new version specifically taps multiple zero and low-emission energy resources for support including nuclear energy, biomass, hydropower, and geothermal energy, just like Obama did, along the emerging field of marine energy, which was barely a speck on the horizon while Obama was in office.

Energy storage, and the critical minerals needed in the clean tech industry, have also been gathered up in a Trumpian embrace.

Conspicuously absent, however, are the two most abundant, economical, and easily accessible domestic energy resources, namely, wind and solar.

If there really was an energy emergency…oh, never mind! Despite Trump’s complaints, offshore wind is a mature industry with a vigorous supply global supply chain and support equipment, as deftly illustrated by the CVOW project. In the October 30 report, Dominion noted that it takes only about two days for an offshore construction vessel to install a wind turbine on its foundation.

While the offshore wind construction timetable is subject to weather delays, Dominion also stated that the project is on schedule — despite any further delays related to last year’s stop-work order.

Speaking of the stop-work order, CVOW was conspicuously excluded from previous rounds of stop-work orders impacting the four other Atlantic coast wind farms under construction in 2025. If you’re guessing that Virginia’s Republican Governor, Glenn Youngkin, had a hand in that decision, drop a note in the discussion thread. Youngkin publicly supported the CVOW project but he was term-limited out of office last year. A stop-work order would have put an embarrassing crimp in his next-step political plans. The order came down only after Election Day 2025, after the Democratic candidate, Abigail Spanberger, won the post.

In a classic case of karma biting back, CVOW is a  more efficient and economical project than initially envisioned. A round of delays during Trump’s first term in office gave Dominion an opportunity to redesign the project with next-generation wind turbines. Those delays also enabled Dominion to take advantage of a new generation of turbine installation vessels that are purpose-built to cut offshore construction costs.

The results speak for themselves. In its October 30 report, Dominion indicated that CVOW costs were still consistent with its capital budget, even accounting for the cost of last year’s stop-work order and costs related to Trump’s tariff wars.

“The estimates on the levelized cost of energy (LCOE) also continue to fall,” Maritime Executive summarized. “After starting with a forecasted range of $80-$90 per MWh, and adjustments for changes after the change in administrations, they have lowered the LCOE to $62, down from an estimate of $83 presented in August” (LCOE is a calculation that provides a common ground for comparing costs among different forms of power generation).

Maritime Executive further reported that Dominion expects to save its ratepayers about $5 billion on fuel over the first 10 years of operation.

Trump’s war on wind has also petered out elsewhere. The Revolution Wind project off the coasts of Connecticut and Massachusetts has also been a target of  multiple stop-work orders over the past two years, with the latest hammer coming down just two weeks ago, when the 704-megawatt wind farm was almost complete.

Revolution Wind is now fully operational, joining the nearby Vineyard Wind project which also endured multiple attacks from Trump. As reported by public radio station WBUR, the savings from Revolution Wind is expected to ripple out through New England, saving $500 million for ratepayers in the region every year.

As for the domestic offshore wind industry writ large, the US lost thousands of onshore supply chain jobs all around the US in addition to job losses related to portside and offshore operation. However, the trade organization Oceantic Network is already prepping for a comeback. “The road to recovery for the embattled American offshore wind industry starts today with a new political mandate to tackle the affordability crisis,” Oceantic President and CEO Liz said in a public statement celebrating the Republican Party’s crushing defeat in the 202y midterm elections.

Photo: The offshore wind construction vessel Charybdis can install wind turbines at Virginia’s CVOW wind farm in just two days (cropped; courtesy of Dominion via CleanTechnica archive).

Magic's Star Trek Already Feels a Lot More Frustrating than Reality Fracture

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

MTG Star Trek Limited is not as smooth as Reality Fracture

A mere six weeks have passed since the release of Reality Fracture, one of the best-received Magic: The Gathering sets in recent years, and players are already primed to enter warp speed with Star Trek. Universes Beyond sets always get their fair share of criticism, and while it’s understandable that not every Magic player is attracted by Star Trek’s unique aesthetic, the most important question for Limited players is: How does the set actually play?

Polygon took part in the 24-hour early access Star Trek event on Magic Arena, and while I only had time for a dozen games between Sealed and Draft, I have one surefire takeaway: this is very different from Reality Fracture.

Limited players loved Reality Fracture for its gameplay and mechanics. Most decks fell into the midrange archetype, focusing on “value” creatures and card advantage to take over the game. The Empower Jace mechanic was the glue of the set, allowing every combination of colors to manipulate the deck through Surveil, thus helping with mana issues and granting the card draw needed to succeed in the format.

Star Trek, by contrast, feels more like The Hobbit. Based on my first impressions, the set encourages aggressive decks that want to curve out early. Every Limited environment tends to favor creatures over spells, but this seems even more true with Star Trek. Even apparently innocuous two-mana creatures like Tenacious Drone proved to be quite annoying during early access, especially when paired with the many “pump” effects available — Sarcophagus, Ship of the Dead, for example, which truly impressed me.

The Rakdos (red-black) Klingon decks seemed very solid, as they can turn up the aggression and also back it up with good removal, which is scarce in the set. Most of the removal spells cost three or more mana or are sorcery-speed. Another strong aggro deck is Gruul (red-green) Landfall, which is able to do truly disgusting things in the first turns of the game. Just look at the pictures below.

On turn 4, my opponent already had nine power on the board (potentially 10 with Planetary Patrol's Landfall effect). On turn 5, they cast a removal spell and attacked me with a total of 14 power thanks to multiple Landfall triggers. If you have a slow-to-medium start, it’s almost impossible to recover from this game state, which leads to my biggest negative impression of Star Trek: the games feel too one-sided.

All of the games I played, both in Sealed and Limited, felt very hard to turn around once one of the players got the advantage. This is typical of aggro formats that rely on curving out and quickly gaining the upper hand. Unfortunately, this can create frustration and make a Limited format grow stale quickly.

By contrast, Reality Fracture still feels fresh (and you can still play it in Arena even while Star Trek events are running) because games are more balanced and usually get decided in the last turns, based on which player has accrued more value and incremental advantage throughout the game. It’s the “classic” Magic midrange playstyle that many players still gravitate towards, even if it’s very hard to find nowadays in Constructed formats.

I drafted this Golgari (green-black) graveyard deck that felt quite strong. This is a strategy that I love to play in every format, so I was confident it could get a decent number of victories. Instead, I went 0-3. The deck was too slow and had no ways to “turn the corner” after the opponent had gained the upper hand.

Also, flooding (drawing too many lands) felt like a real issue, despite the many Surveil cards I played. I conceded a game on turn five simply because I had drawn too many lands, and my opponent was already too far ahead on the board for me to catch up. The lack of a set-wide way to mitigate bad draws is unpleasant, but I guess this is the post-Empower Jace effect. Can’t we just have that in every set?

I’m not implying that Star Trek will be an aggro-only format. Unlike The Hobbit, this is a large set, and the card pool is big enough that players can successfully build different strategies. For example, I had success with this five-color Sealed deck that simply played all the bombs, removal, fixing, and card draw I had in my pool. It was really fun. Even then, I consistently struggled to stay alive until I could deploy my bombs.

On the other hand, this simple Klingon deck also won a lot of games. It’s aggressive, has a lot of removal, and is supported by some synergies and card advantage engines to make sure you don’t run out of things to do. Red feels like a strong color in Star Trek, and it can support three of the four main aggressive archetypes.

If you’re heading to a prerelease event this weekend, my suggestion is to go back to the basics for this set. Focus on having a good curve, play a lot of creatures, and all the removal you can. Splashing is easy thanks to the many multicolored lands, but I would be wary of playing too many lands that enter tapped. As always, remember to have fun! May the Force be with you! (Did I get that right?)

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

California schools must drop ultra-processed food, and kitchen managers explain why that’s hard

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

California will phase ultra-processed foods out of school meals, but kitchen directors say money, staff and small kitchens stand in the way.

Taking junk food off school menus sounds like a matter of writing a rule. California has written one, and starting in 2029 its schools must begin phasing out many ultra-processed foods.

But when researchers asked the people who run school kitchens what makes cooking from scratch hard, the answers were money, staff and kitchens built mostly to reheat.

Many districts even buy basic ingredients, then pay to have them turned into the processed foods that the law targets.

That’s the gap between a law and a lunch line, and the directors said closing it will take more than a deadline.

The Real Food, Healthy Kids Act requires California schools to begin phasing out certain ultra-processed foods in July 2029 and finish by 2035. These are factory-made foods with ingredients that home cooks rarely use, such as flavorings, colorings and emulsifiers.

Such foods make up about two-thirds of the calories U.S. children eat, according to studies the authors cite. School meals can supply about half of a child’s daily calories, which makes the cafeteria a big lever on what kids eat.

Andrea Pedroza-Tobias, then an instructor at Stanford Medicine and now at UC Irvine, led the study with pediatrician Anisha Patel.

Community groups, including the Dolores Huerta Foundation and Cultiva La Salud, helped shape it, along with the Nutrition Policy Institute at the University of California.

From January to June 2024, the team interviewed food service directors from 20 school districts in California’s San Joaquin Valley, a farming region with many low-income families.

The districts were picked at random from urban and rural areas, and from districts with and without a state grant for freshly prepared meals.

Sixteen of the directors also filled out a short survey. On average, their districts served some scratch-cooked items about 2.5 days a week and heat-and-serve foods about 3 days a week.

That is a small group in one region. The authors note that the results may not match other parts of the country, and that directors who agreed to talk may care more about scratch cooking than those who didn’t.

Two-thirds of the surveyed directors identified staff shortages and a lack of money for labor as obstacles. Many rely on part-time workers who can reheat food but don’t have time to cook it. Storage and kitchen space came next.

State grants for kitchen equipment helped, but the money went different ways. Some districts bought skillets and ranges for cooking, while others bought more ovens to assist with reheating. Directors also said one-year grants made it hard to plan for the long run.

Most did credit California’s free meals for every student with bringing in more money, and in some districts more cooks.

In an interview with Earth.com, Patel and Pedroza-Tobias said the money has to come with guidelines for using it. “However, funding alone is not enough,” they said. They pointed to policies, practices and technical assistance paired with funding.

Schools get raw ingredients through a federal food program, but many districts ship them out to be processed. “I’m sending my chicken to, let’s say, Tyson and getting chicken patties and chicken nuggets,” one director told the researchers.

Directors also said most products labeled and sold for schools, the ones built to meet federal nutrition rules, are ultra-processed.

Amanda Harvey, who runs nutrition services for Fresno Unified and sat on the study’s advisory board, has seen supply problems firsthand. When her district dropped products with food dye, pickles made without green dye became hard to find.

“They’re pricey for pickles, and it can be quite challenging for our vendor to get them,” Harvey said. Another director recalled spending years removing cooking equipment and buying more processed food, and now using grant money to buy the same kinds of kettles and skillets back.

Some directors worried students would skip scratch-cooked meals and throw more food away. Others said students preferred fresh, familiar food, and they described ways to win kids over, including taste tests, family nutrition nights and student recipe contests.

Asked by Earth.com what surprised them most, the researchers pointed to districts that found creative ways around tight budgets. “One food service director even dressed in costumes to encourage children to try healthier foods,” they said.

They also noted that at least seven other states have passed laws on specific food additives or dyes, though most still allow some ultra-processed foods. Brazil’s national school meal program, they said, requires at least 85 percent of federal food funds to go to fresh or minimally processed foods.

The directors described what they expect and what they’ve tried. The study didn’t measure what children actually ate, how their health changed, or whether the new law’s timeline is realistic.

The authors call for money to hire and keep staff, upgrade kitchens and train cooks, along with rules that make ultra-processed products less attractive to buy.

Whether California’s districts can make that switch by 2035 will show up on real trays over the next decade, and no one has measured it yet.

Research on kids’ behavior and toddler foods has kept attention on processed food in childhood, and on its health risks. Schools may be where the change is easiest to track.

The full study was published in the journal Public Health Nutrition.

A virtual escape may help children feel less scared before surgery

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Children reported less fear after brief virtual reality sessions before surgery, offering early evidence for a possible drug-free calming option.

Before an operation, plenty of children are scared, and a common way to calm them is a sedative that carries risks of its own.

Researchers tested a drug-free option: a virtual reality headset showing a calm playroom or a quiet beach.

After a short session in it, children said they felt less upset and less scared, while their heart rates went down.

Whether the headset deserves the credit is still an open question.

Walaa Abu Rukbah, a pharmacist at the University of Tabuk in Saudi Arabia, is the study’s first author.

“Sometimes, the best support we can offer a child before surgery isn’t a medicine at all,” said Abu Rukbah.

The project ran for nine months at a children’s hospital in Virginia. Every child in it used the headset, so there was no group of children who waited without one to compare against.

Between half and three-quarters of children feel anxious before surgery, by the researchers’ estimate.

A scared child isn’t only unhappy. Very anxious children are more likely to struggle when it’s time to go under anesthesia.

They’re also more likely to wake up confused and agitated, a state doctors call emergence delirium, and to have more pain and a slower recovery.

The calming drug used most often is midazolam. It can make some children more agitated instead of calmer, and it can slow breathing or delay the trip home.

Drug-free options such as play therapy and music have helped children in earlier studies, but they need trained staff that not every hospital has.

The team spent 10 months designing its own virtual world for children.

The main scene is a playroom in soft blues and greens, with toys that respond when a child looks at them and slow music in the background.

A second scene shows a beach with waves and palm trees.

Some people experience motion sickness in virtual reality because what they see does not match the motion their body senses.

To avoid that, children hop from spot to spot instead of gliding around. When 15 children tried it early on, none of them reported feeling sick.

From October 2023 to June 2024, 80 children aged 6 to 15 used the headset while waiting for planned surgery at Children’s Hospital of Richmond at VCU.

Their parents could watch on a tablet what the child was seeing. A typical session lasted about nine minutes, though some ran as long as 20.

Before and after each session, children rated how upset they felt from 0 to 10, much like the pain scales doctors use.

They also pointed to one of five faces, ranging from calm to very scared.

The typical child’s score dropped from 3 to 1 on the 0-to-10 scale. On the faces, the typical child pointed to the second face before the session and the calmest face after it.

Not every child changed much. By the team’s measure of a change big enough to matter, 46 of the 80 children felt noticeably less upset afterward and 44 felt noticeably less scared. That’s a little more than half.

Bedside monitors picked up a change too. Heart rate dropped by nearly six beats a minute on average.

Systolic blood pressure – the top number in a blood pressure reading – fell by about 4 points on average.

Only three children had any side effects, and all of them were minor.

Dayanjan Wijesinghe, a researcher in the Department of Pharmacotherapy and Outcomes Science at Virginia Commonwealth University (VCU), was also involved in the study.

“In a busy children’s surgical unit, a short virtual reality session proved easy for nurses to run, was safe, and was very popular: nine in ten children wanted to use it again, and nearly every parent would recommend it,” said Wijesinghe.

All 29 nurses involved said they would recommend it too.

When parents wrote about the experience, they mentioned distraction more than anything else.

“He was distracted and didn’t notice when the needle went in,” one parent wrote.

Not every parent was equally enthusiastic.

“If my child chose it, sure. To me it’s a bit overwhelming to have so much going on,” another parent wrote.

Waiting rooms can get easier on their own. A child might calm down simply from getting used to the room, or from having an adult pay close attention for a while.

The most scared children had the biggest drops.

However, unusually high initial scores can fall on a second measurement even without an effective treatment, potentially making the headset appear more helpful.

Scientists call this regression to the mean, and it can make health programs look better than they really are.

The team’s own checks suggested it explains much of why the most anxious children improved the most.

Families also knew they were trying something new, and the study team collected their ratings in person. That can lead people to give kinder answers.

Once the children left the waiting room, the researchers stopped tracking them.

Whether a calmer wait leads to an easier start to anesthesia or a faster recovery wasn’t measured.

All of the children came from a single hospital, so a hospital with different staff or patients might see different results.

In the researchers’ view, the next step is a randomized trial that compares the headset with usual care and with other drug-free options.

Such a trial could also show whether a headset costs less than giving children a sedative.

The full study was published in the journal PLOS Digital Health.

Check us out on EarthSnap, a free app brought to you by Eric Ralls and Earth.com.

Fox Corp. Completes $22 Billion Deal for Roku

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There is a new sheriff in Roku City: Fox has completed its purchase of the streaming and tech company.

The $22 billion deal closed today, combining Fox Corp.’s broadcast, news, sports, streaming, and local news portfolio with Roku’s open connected TV platform and first-party data from more than 100 million global streaming households.

The merged company becomes the third-largest entity in U.S. television by share of viewing. Fox says the addition of Roku will create new opportunities to serve its viewers, advertisers, and partners.

Executive chair and CEO of Fox Corp. Lachlan Murdoch said acquiring Roku advances the media company’s long-term strategy and will continue to “relentlessly serve audiences and partners, while reinforcing the openness and choice that is the essence of the Roku platform.”

Roku will be folded into Fox Digital Platforms, which also houses fellow FAST service Tubi, Fox One, and Red Seat Ventures. The streaming platform’s founder, Anthony Wood, will join Fox’s board of directors and serve as co-chair of Fox Digital Platforms alongside Paul Cheesbrough, the unit’s CEO.

When the merger was announced in June, the companies said they were looking to create a “scaled next-generation media and technology company” to prosper in today’s environment of increasing consumption of live sports, news, and streaming.

Roku stockholders will receive $95.30 in cash and 0.9799 shares of Fox Class A common stock for each share of Roku Class A and Roku Class B common stock they owned before the deal closed, under the merger agreement.

Separately, Fox shareholders will now own approximately 74% of the outstanding shares of Fox common stock, and the remaining approximately 26% will go to former Roku shareholders.

Reflecting on Roku’s acquisition, Woods said in a statement that he was proud of what his team has built and that combining with Fox provides an opportunity to accelerate its vision, scale faster, and be more aggressive in innovation for its respective audiences.

Woods added, “I couldn’t be more excited about what we’ll accomplish together.”

3 Elite Netflix Series to Binge This Weekend (Nov 7-8)

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The most-watched show on American Netflix is, once again, a piece of non-fiction, as viewers from across the country explore the life and loss of Friends favorite Matthew Perry. The One About Matthew Perry is a brand-new three-part docuseries exploring the incredibly impressive and sadly tragic life of Perry, who passed away on October 28, 2023, at the age of 54. Directed by Mary Robertson, this acclaimed docuseries is the show to watch right now on Netflix. But what about the rest of the streamer's catalog? Here's a look at three shows you should binge-watch on Netflix this weekend.

For more recommendations, check out our list of the best shows and movies on Netflix.

Disclaimer: These titles are available on US Netflix.

Fans of The Lincoln Lawyer won't want to miss out on this ABC police procedural that has been added to the streamer's catalog this week. Will Trent stars Ramón Rodríguez as the titular Special Agent, who was let down by Atlanta's foster-care system as a child. Now at the GBI, he uses his unique experience to ensure no one faces the same trauma that he did.

Based on Karin Slaughter's bestselling books, Will Trent has been one of the most quietly acclaimed procedurals on network TV for the past three years. In that time, four seasons of must-watch crime drama have been released, with a fifth scheduled to premiere as part of the midseason lineup in January next year. Will Trent is one of the most exciting new Netflix additions and should be at the top of your watchlist.

A great detective is really just a personality with a method attached, which is why we keep remaking the same four of them. Here is yours, what it says about you, and the case for them being the best in the business.

You let people think you are not a threat and then you simply never go away. Columbo is the only major detective show that tells you who did it in the first ten minutes, because the pleasure was never the puzzle, it was watching a rich man slowly realise he is in trouble.

You are the most interesting person in the room and you know exactly how to use it. Rian Johnson built Blanc as a gentleman detective who is also an outsider everywhere he goes, which lets him be charming at people who would never otherwise talk to him.

Everybody tells you everything, and you have never once asked them to. Jessica Fletcher is a crime novelist from a small town in Maine who is so warm and so obviously trustworthy that suspects confess to her essentially as a social courtesy.

You expect to be lied to and you are almost never disappointed. Chandler wrote Marlowe as a man with a rigid private code in a city that has none, which is why he takes the case, solves it, gets nothing out of it and takes the next one.

Earlier this week, one of the most intense shows on Netflix made its explosive return after two years away. Black Doves stars Keira Knightley as Helen Webb, who is placed under the protection of an old friend (Ben Whishaw) after her husband, the UK's Secretary of State for Defence (Andrew Buchan), is murdered.

Following a must-watch first season, the second is ready to turn up the tension dial. "Helen is still betraying her nation’s secrets to the covert organization she serves," according to a second-season synopsis, although "she’s walking a more treacherous line than ever" as her husband prepares to become Prime Minister. Writer Joe Barton's stylish, detailed spy thriller dazzled audiences in 2024 and will do so again this weekend.

Created by Stacey Aglok MacDonald and Alethea Arnaquq-Baril, the Canadian comedy North of North has been one of Netflix's best hidden gems since it debuted last year. Earlier this week, the second season arrived, so now is the best time to catch up on what you might have missed, as young Inuk woman Siaja (Anna Lambe) reinvents her image after a public breakup.

Easy to watch and packed with heartfelt comedy, North of North isn't the most demanding series available on Netflix, thriving as comfort viewing for its many fans. Maika Harper, Braeden Clarke, Bailey Poching, and more also star alongside Lambe in this Canadian favorite, which scored a perfect 100% rating on review aggregator Rotten Tomatoes after its first season.

Lucid teases its cheaper sub-$50,000 Cosmos EV in new video and images

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Lucid has released a new teaser video of what looks like the Cosmos, its upcoming sub-$50,000 midsize electric SUV and the car that’s supposed to turn Lucid into a volume automaker.

The clip gives us the clearest look yet at the Cosmos’ lighting, front and rear, after months of camouflaged prototype sightings. But the car itself is still about a year away.

Lucid posted the 1 minute 34 second video on X yesterday. The caption talks about “elevating the experience of movement” and says Lucid can’t wait “to share this feeling with even more people.” It doesn’t name the vehicle, but “even more people” is about as subtle a hint at a cheaper model as you can get.

Two frames from the film show the vehicle at dusk. At the rear, “LUCID” is spelled out in red illuminated letters across the tailgate, flanked by thin red light strips, with a spoiler cutting across the base of the rear glass. Up front, a sculpted LED headlight with a row of vertical segments sweeps across a red body panel.

That rear end matches the Cosmos design Lucid registered with the EU Intellectual Property Office in May, which showed the same spelled-out “LUCID” tailgate and rear-glass spoiler. It also lines up with the thin red light strips on the camouflaged Cosmos prototype spotted on Highway 101 in California last month.

Lucid unveiled its plans for Cosmos and Earth at its first Investor Day in March. Both ride on the new midsize platform and start under $50,000, roughly half the price of the Gravity.

On paper, the Cosmos is impressive. Lucid claims about 300 miles of range from a 69 kWh pack, thanks to efficiency of up to 4.5 mi/kWh, which it says is 10% better than its closest competitor. It also claims 200 miles of range in 14 minutes of charging, and its new Atlas drive unit has 30% fewer parts and costs 37% less than the Zeus unit in the Gravity.

The problem is timing. Production was supposed to start in late 2026. In August, CEO Silvio Napoli pushed the Cosmos to 2027, “most likely the second half.”

That puts it on the same schedule as Rivian’s $45,000 base R2, and well after the Tesla Model Y it was caught testing next to in Arizona back in May.

Lucid needs this car badly. It delivered 3,806 vehicles in Q3, down 7% year-over-year, while building only 2,954 after cutting the second shift at its Arizona factory.

The company is building hype a year out. It has nothing else new to sell until the Cosmos arrives, and the company reports Q3 earnings on Monday. They need to remind everyone what they are working toward.

A midsize SUV doing 4.5 mi/kWh would be best in class by a wide margin, and Lucid’s efficiency claims have held up on the Air. If it really lands under $50,000 with 300 miles of range, it’s a very compelling alternative to the Model Y and the R2, which still have room to take over market shares from ICE vehicles.

But Lucid has already slipped this car once; it says its cash lasts “well into 2027,” and the Cosmos is due in the second half of 2027. That’s a tight overlap. I think the Cosmos will be a good car, but can Lucid fund it all the way to customers’ driveways?

Welcome back to Conformity Gate. With Stranger Things Day sending us all back to the Upside Down today, Sony has the headphones to pair with that world-saving Walkman. The brand has just announced a Stranger Things edition of the new MDR-3R wired headphones, now available with bold red cushions (and a black set included in the box). The $79 / £59 Stranger Things variant bumps up the price of the new release a little (the standard model starts at $59 / £49) but maintains a style fit for Hawkins.

The Sony MDR-3R headphones are being described as "Newtro" by the brand, combining more modern audio handling (and a USB-C connection) with the instantly recognizable retro aesthetic. Sony has faithfully reconstructed the MDR-3 design, with that skinny band, chunky silver driver plate, and simple design. If we ever needed another reason to start running up that hill, this is it.

The new Sony MDR-3Rs are still waiting to go live on the brand's own site, though notifications are available if you want to be one of the first in the line. It's worth noting that the red-cushion Stranger Things version is a limited edition, so keep that refresh button warm.

So what does the Stranger Things edition bring to its higher price tag? It's a subtle one. The blue / orange cushion color options of the standard model are replaced with a deep Upside Down red and black pairing with a Stranger Things logo stamped at the top of the headband.

If you're going for the full '80s aesthetic, you'd be better off with the orange base option, saving yourself some cash in the process. If you're going like-for-like with Max herself, you'll need the limited edition to get access to those black pads.

The headphones themselves are constructed from a lightweight aluminum and stainless steel, powered by that USB-C connection for a charge-free experience, and still feature an inline remote with an integrated microphone for media control and calls.

Sony says it's kept the MDR-3R sound profile as close to the original as possible, while still elevating sound with some more modern features. That open-back design is still going to give your music plenty more breathing space, but the brand has extended both lower and upper ranges for a more dynamic quality.

While the absence of that ninth episode still continues to hurt, at least we've got some tunes to see us through. Plus, with Maya Hawke joining the Magic: The Gathering movie there's still plenty from the series stars in the works.

Real Madrid head coach Jose Mourinho addresses Vinicius Junior's struggles at Bernabeu

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

By Matt Law | 06 Nov 2026 13:41, Last updated: 06 Nov 2026 13:44

Real Madrid head coach Jose Mourinho remains confident that Vinicius Junior will rediscover his top form for the Spanish giants in the not too distant future.

Vinicius will complete a four-game domestic suspension on Saturday, when Real Madrid continue their La Liga campaign against Valencia.

The 26-year-old has had a tough campaign to date, only managing one goal and three assists in 12 appearances for Los Blancos in all competitions.

Vinicius again struggled to make his mark against AEK Athens in the Champions League, with the statistics showing how difficult a game it was for the Brazilian.

The attacker will once again be available in La Liga after the November international break, and Mourinho has said that he will continue to help Vinicius rediscover his best form.

“For me, the most important thing is the team. Never by my own will would I harm the team to benefit a player. When we return to normal with Vini Jr. being available, I’ll manage it," Mourinho told reporters during Friday's press conference.

"I want the team to think game by game, but as a coach, I have to think about the next matches and the sequence of games we have. The match against AEK was for Vini Jr., to give some rest to a player like [Arda] Guler, who had some issues.

“The most straightforward answer to your question is that a coach must do everything to have his players at the highest level, try different strategies, in-game or personal management, and always believe in the player’s true potential.

"A bad player will always be bad no matter how much you try to improve them. A good one has their bad moments but leaves the door open to return to their normal state.

"The game that Vini Jr. played in Athens - if it wasn’t Vini Jr. but a regular player - we’d all say he had a good game. Not incredible because his team didn’t play incredibly.

"We always expect the Vini Jr. who eliminated me from the Champions League, and that version exists, which we all expect. We must continue working with all strategies at all levels.

"Something important is the friendship of his teammates, without exception. When deciding who plays, the team is above the individual.”

The South American has scored 129 goals and registered 103 assists in 387 matches for Real Madrid during a highly-successful period.

Vinicius had been heavily linked with a move to Arsenal during the summer transfer window before ultimately signing a new long-term contract at Bernabeu.

TeraWulf’s bitcoin mining revenue falls 69% as HPC leasing surges amid Anthropic buildout

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The former bitcoin mining-focused company now generates most of its revenue from high-performance computing, with further data center capacity under construction.

TeraWulf's TeraWulf, Inc. (WULF) bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) mining revenue fell to $13.2 million in the third quarter from $43.4 million a year earlier, a decline of about 69%, while the Nasdaq-listed company reported $63 million in high-performance computing (HPC) lease revenue, up 774% from $7.2 million in the same period last year.

Total revenue came to $76.2 million, an increase of 51% year over year, according to its latest earnings release on Friday, with HPC leasing accounting for 83% of that revenue compared to just 14% a year earlier.

The company reported $709.9 million in net income attributable to shareholders for Q3, including a $728.4 million non-cash gain from changes in the fair value of warrant and derivative liabilities. Its operating loss widened to $88.2 million from $24.7 million a year ago, while adjusted EBITDA fell to negative $12.8 million.

TeraWulf said it had 102 MW of critical IT capacity generating revenue at its Lake Mariner site in New York as of Sept. 30, while construction is underway on another 336 MW. TeraWulf expects the latest data center building at the site to start generating rent in the fourth quarter and another in early 2027.

TeraWulf previously agreed to a 20-year lease with Anthropic for about 401 MW of critical IT capacity at its Justified campus in Kentucky. The agreement covers approximately $19 billion in contracted revenue over its initial term. Earlier this week, the company announced a Google credit backstop for project financing in support of Anthropic's lease and a definitive engineering, procurement, and construction agreement with Fluor Enterprises, covering three data center buildings. The first phases are scheduled for delivery in late 2027.

Meanwhile, at its Muskie campus in Kentucky, TeraWulf's contracted power capacity has increased from 500 MW to 1 GW, pending regulatory approval.

"Lake Mariner is delivering contracted capacity and generating revenue, Justified has the customer, credit support and construction framework to move forward at scale, and Muskie gives us a contracted path to a gigawatt of power," TeraWulf Chairman and CEO Paul Prager said in the earnings release.

Across its portfolio, the company is targeting another 250 MW to 500 MW of contracted critical IT capacity each year. TeraWulf said it had about $2.4 billion in cash, cash equivalents, and restricted cash at the end of the quarter.

TeraWulf shares are up 8.1% at $10.90 in pre-market trading on Friday following the release.

OpenAI has launched GPT-6, a cybersecurity AI model that delivers "major gains" on key cybersecurity benchmarks over its predecessor, GPT-5.6 Cyber.

GPT-6 Cyber is "already helping to secure real-world software, uncovering and validating thousands of exploits in security-critical codebases such as Linux and Firefox," said the post.

Tesla to wind down Cybertruck production

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Tesla will wind down Cybertruck production at its Texas gigafactory around mid-2027, CEO Elon Musk said on Friday, after years of persistently weak sales for a futuristic model once touted as a rival to legacy pickup trucks that ultimately only sold in just a handful of markets because of its heavy, angular design.

Musk said in an X post the production wind-down would enable the automaker to accommodate increased output of its newly launched Cybercab model, which the billionaire has made central to Tesla's robotaxi ambitions.

While it was not immediately clear whether Cybertruck production would be moved to another facility or phased out entirely, the automaker's Austin plant is the only known Tesla facility equipped to manufacture the model's specialized components.

In response to a user on X, Musk called the Cybertruck the "best vehicle Tesla has ever made" and the wind-down the "end of an era."

Tesla did not immediately respond to a Reuters request for comment sent outside regular business hours.

The US EV maker only began delivering the Cybertruck to customers in late 2023. According to Kelley Blue Book, the company only sold 20,237 Cybertrucks in the US last year, down 48% from 38,965 in 2024. Before the pickup truck launched, Musk had predicted annual sales of 250,000 units.

"It doesn't make sense to keep it going when it's not meeting its targets," said former Aston Martin CEO Andy Palmer. "Every rational auto guy would say the same."

"It will no doubt only be a rounding error on Tesla's balance sheet, but the statistics speak for themselves."

Right from its unveiling in 2019, the Cybertruck was a controversial choice for Musk. Its angular design was mocked by pickup truck purists and safety experts predicted it would be hard to gain approval for sale in key markets like Europe.

The unveiling event itself was marred when Musk tried to prove that its windows were shatterproof by having metal balls thrown at them - only to leave massive cracks in two of them.

During Cybertruck's official launch in 2023, Musk touted it as a futuristic competitor to pickup trucks from General Motors and Ford, saying "finally, the future will look like the future."

But the vehicle has struggled to meet those expectations.

Automotive analyst Automotive analyst Felipe Munoz said that although the Cybertruck has been called a failure because of its poor sales, electric pick-ups from Ford and others have also flopped in the US market.

"All of them lost the competition because electric pick ups aren't popular in the US," Munoz said.

But Cybertruck's sharp-edged stainless-steel design limited its prospects in markets like Europe where EVs are more popular. Regulatory approval was impossible for Europe because of rules there concerning pedestrian impact.

"Anywhere where you have crash regulation that takes into account pedestrian impact, that Cybertruck won't go, can't go," former Aston Martin CEO Palmer said.

The Cybercab, which has butterfly doors and no steering wheel, pedals or mirrors, is one of Musk's key bets in his push to turn Tesla into an AI and robotics company rather than just an automaker.

Musk has said he sees it as Tesla's main autonomous model supplying a global network of robotaxis.

After years of delays to Musk's self-driving targets, Tesla began offering paid rides in a small fleet of Cybercabs in Austin in September. However, the automaker still has to answer the US auto safety agency's questions on whether it has properly self-certified Cybercab to demonstrate compliance with federal motor vehicle safety standards.

"Cybertruck was an incredible technology development platform and lives on through Cybercab," Cybertruck lead engineer Wes Morrill said on X.

Six pallets of Nvidia GPUs worth $11.4 million stolen in California cargo heist

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As high-end PC hardware continues to surge in demand and price, GPU theft has become a highly profitable target for criminals. According to a report by ABC7 News, authorities are investigating a major cargo heist involving the disappearance of an $11.4 million shipment of Nvidia hardware. The shipment reportedly consisted of six pallets of Nvidia hardware that left Oakland for Texas on August 29 and was picked up by a truck posing as a food transport company. Investigators eventually lost track of the vehicle in San Bernardino County, with the Oakland Police Department receiving a report of the theft on September 13.

"These chips command about double the price that they command inside the United States. So, if a chip costs about $550,000, then they go for about 1 million or more abroad," said Olaf Groth, a professor at UC Berkeley's Haas School of Business.

While there has been no announcement regarding any arrests, the authorities are said to be investigating the matter. The incident has also raised concerns among experts about the security of high-value technology shipments and the potential national security risks posed by advanced computing hardware making its way into black-market channels.

In a similar incident from Brazil back in September, two pallets containing Nvidia's top-of-the-line GeForce RTX 5090 graphics cards, valued at approximately $470,000 (R$2.5 million), disappeared at Guarulhos International Airport in São Paulo. The shipment, imported from the US, had reportedly arrived at the airport on September 20 and cleared customs on September 24, but the two pallets went missing the following day after being authorized for release.

The investigation eventually uncovered a possible inside job, with Brazilian police identifying three airport employees suspected of helping carry out the heist. According to reports, two outsourced forklift operators allegedly removed the pallets from a warehouse, an incident that was reportedly captured on CCTV, while a third employee responsible for checking and releasing merchandise allegedly helped bypass the airport's electronic controls.

Things took an interesting turn when one of the stolen Zotac RTX 5090 graphics cards appeared for sale on the Brazilian online marketplace OLX for more than $6,000. The listing included clear pictures of the card's serial number, allowing it to be linked to the missing shipment. The listing has since been taken down, while the whereabouts of the remaining graphics cards remain unclear.

Hollywood Prediction Market Faces Insider Scare: James Bond Casting Bets Shift

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Hollywood studios are tightening restrictions on employees betting on prediction market after suspicious activity surrounding the next James Bond casting decision. The prediction market controversy intensified after a platform temporarily pulled its market following a sudden wave of bets backing Callum Turner.

The Hollywood Reporter reported that studios and production companies have been quietly warning employees against wagering on confidential industry information. The restrictions cover casting decisions, movie release dates, and studio earnings. At least one company has reportedly established an anonymous hotline for reporting suspected insider betting.

THR has learned studios and production companies have been quietly issuing new guidelines to employees warning them not to engage in prediction market betting.• The warnings prohibit employees from placing bets on news events like studio earnings, casting, or even release… pic.twitter.com/JbiovSwZC9

The crackdown follows growing interest in entertainment contracts on platforms such as Polymarket and Kalshi. Employees could potentially turn confidential production information into profits before announcements reach the public. They are also being warned against passing information to friends or relatives who could place bets on their behalf.

A prediction market platform temporarily suspended its next James Bond casting market after a sudden wave of sharp money pushed Callum Turner ahead of longtime favorite Jack Lowden. The movement raised concerns that traders might have received information about the casting decision before an official announcement.

Turner’s odds had already improved following casting rumors and reports of screen tests. However, the sudden betting activity attracted additional attention, particularly because Amazon MGM Studios has yet to announce its next 007.

Domer who has made over $2,000,000 trading prediction markets doesn't believe Callum Turner will be the next James Bond despite him being the current favorite“Now Callum Turner, he's at like 75 cents off some rumors or whatever”“Basically I'm betting on none of the current… pic.twitter.com/9cEJXr3yde

Kalshi’s November 6 market snapshot showed Turner leading with a 62% implied probability. Josh O’Connor followed at 18%, while Lowden held 14%. Prediction market has become an increasingly attractive target for traders seeking opportunities beyond politics and sports, such as Hollywood.

Entertainment contracts can cover award winners, reality television eliminations, celebrity appearances, and confidential production developments. The potential for insider betting is particularly concerning when employees have access to information that ordinary participants cannot obtain.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

The controversy also highlights the infrastructure demands facing blockchain applications as prediction markets expand into entertainment and other real-world events. Faster execution and affordable transactions could become increasingly important as developers explore new onchain financial products.

Bitcoin Layer 2 projects are pursuing similar infrastructure improvements. Bitcoin Hyper uses the Solana Virtual Machine (SVM) to support high-throughput execution, lower transaction costs, and programmable applications while periodically settling Layer 2 state to Bitcoin.

Too fast. Too strong. Too Hyper. 🔥⚡️https://t.co/VNG0P4GuDo pic.twitter.com/oLavMFuU7w

It currently records more than $33.2 million raised, with HYPER priced at just $0.0136. The project also offers 35% staking rewards, while its November 26 presale deadline approaches.

The development effort aims to extend Bitcoin beyond traditional transfers and long-term storage. Its proposed ecosystem includes decentralized finance, payments, and other applications that require faster transaction processing.

Hollywood restrictions show a difficult enforcement problem for prediction market platforms such as Polymarket and Kalshi. Blockchain transactions can provide publicly accessible records of wallet activity, but identifying the person controlling an address is another matter. Traders can also use multiple wallets and intermediaries to make their activity harder to connect to their real identities.

The issue is not entirely theoretical. Kalshi previously fined a video editor working for MrBeast’s company $20,000 after the employee reportedly generated approximately $5,000 through bets using confidential information. Beast Industries subsequently fired the employee.

Kalshi Sues Mr Beast Editor for insider trading on the beast game ‘They insider traded about $4k,was Mr beast aware of his editor making bet of his show’ ‘imagine people doing this with anime predicting the outcome of the anime whereas there is already a manga’A lot of… pic.twitter.com/yCyu4r0GiD

Federal prosecutors have also pursued separate prediction-market cases involving an active-duty soldier and a Google engineer. Both cases involve allegations of using confidential information to place bets.

Hollywood restricting employees from betting in prediction market is only the first step, and the next James Bond announcement may finally settle the casting speculation.

A Chinese inquiry halted OPay’s $304M US IPO, Bloomberg reports

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Nigeria-focused fintech’s only tie to China is its founder, Zhou Yahui. Bankers on the deal were shocked, Semafor reported, and a valuation of about $4bn is at stake.

A Chinese regulator’s inquiry led OPay to halt its $304M New York listing hours after pricing it, Bloomberg reported. The Nigeria-focused payments company has no operations or revenue in China, according to the report.

Loni Prinsloo and Julia Fioretti reported for Bloomberg that OPay’s only link to China is its founder, Chinese businessman Zhou Yahui. They cited people familiar with the matter. Talks are ongoing, the people said, and no one knows yet if the two sides can settle it. OPay declined to comment to Bloomberg, and the China Securities Regulatory Commission did not respond.

Zhou also founded Kunlun Tech, which once owned the dating app Grindr. In 2016 he led a group that bought part of Norway’s Opera.

OPay filed for listing in New York in October. It sold 16 million American depositary shares at $19 each, the bottom of its $19 to $21 range, Semafor reported. The shares never started trading.

OPay put out a statement on 31 October. It did not name the country behind the inquiry.

The inquiry relates to “a jurisdiction that is immaterial to the Company’s core business operations,” OPay said.

The company said it was confident the inquiry would end well soon. OPay and its banks agreed to end their underwriting deal of 29 October. It said it would share updates as they come.

People close to the deal told Semafor’s Yinka Adegoke that the move shocked bankers and advisers.

“A disaster for everyone,” one of them told Semafor.

According to Semafor, OPay’s prospectus warned investors it was unclear if it needed Beijing’s approval to list in the US. Citing its Chinese lawyers, the company said it did not believe it needed the regulator’s approval.

OPay’s prospectus listed China International Capital Corp among its underwriters, alongside Citigroup, Deutsche Bank and Standard Bank. Bloomberg reported that CICC later came off the deal.

OPay has its headquarters in Singapore but makes most of its money in Nigeria. The country brought in 89.5% of its revenue in the first half of 2026, its prospectus shows. OPay had 50.1 million monthly users at the end of July. It also serves Egypt, Indonesia and Pakistan.

Bloomberg said a valuation of about $4bn is at stake. It tied the case to Beijing’s moves against firms outside China. One was forcing Meta to undo its $2bn purchase of Manus. Beijing blocked that deal, and Manus has since raised more than $500M on its own.
